894.24/41
I have the honor to report, as of possible
interest to the Department, that E. Hirasawa of
the Toa Kigyo Company of Tokio, Japan visited
Belize during the past month and while here made
arrangements for a shipment of mahogany lumber to
MAR 4 - 1932
Japan, the avowed use for which is the making of
FILED
airplane propellers. It is understood that the
amount purchased was approximately 50,000 sq. feet,
and that it is to be delivered in Vancouver, B.C.
during Maroh and April, 1932.
Well informed persons state that this is the
first shipment of mahogany from British Honduras to
Japan. Mr. Hirasawa advised local mahogany
contractors that he formerly secured such mahogany
from the United States but that, in view of the
diffieulty of securing the proper quality there, he
found

=== Page 3 === (confidence 0.95)
- 2 -
found it more convenient to purchase here. When
asked why metal propellers were not used he is
said to have stated that mahogany ones were more
suitable for military purposes.
Mr. Hirasawa left Belize for Kingston, Jamaica
on February 2, 1952.
Respectfully yours,
Reaciv.
Robert M. Ott,
American Vice Consul.

Buwl
I have the honor to inform the Department that I
have learned from sources ∞nsidered as reliable that
Japanese interests are purchasing quantities of supplies
in various parts of the Republic of Mexioo. It has been
learned that Algodones, S. A., controlled by Anderson-
Clayton, have bought ootton to the value of several million
dollars for shipment to Japan. Presumably, the Imperial
Valley will furnish a goodly proportion. The cotton fromy
13
will
1MAR5
be shipped via the port of Manzanillo.
It is also reported the Japanese purohasing agents
are located at Guaymas, Hermosillo, Mazatlan and other
points on the West Coast, for the aoquisition of dried
shrimps and other seafood for shipment to Japan.
The firm of Sykes and Wooddell, important Amerioan
stookmen

=== Page 6 === (confidence 0.98)
-2
stookmen operating in southern Arizona and northern
Sonora, are shipping 2000 three and four-year-old
steers of Mexican origin to Ciudad Obregon where they
will be fattened on green wheat and peas, and made
ready for the Mexico City market; or if that market
should prove unsatisfaotory and the Japanese Government
is still in the market for cattle, they may be shipped
to Japan. It is learned that the Japanese diplomatio
mission at Mexico has recently made inquiries with
regard to the availability of cattle for shipment to
Japan. In view of the unsatisfactory cattle markets
in Mexioo and in the United States the Sonora cattlemen
are naturally very muoh interested in the possible
opening up of markets in the Orient.
Respectfully yours,
Anerican gonaul

Following from Consul General at Nanking:
894.24/44
"March 2, 10 a.n.
One. A reliable Chinese informant tells me that
the Ch:nen generally belicve that th rican Government
has recently sold to the Japanese Go rnnent 1.80,000,000
worth of munitions.
They resent such a sale while Japan
is seeking to oppress China and one explanation offered
is that the United States is trying to bring about the
MAR 9 - 1932
duplication of the Asiatic pyretic through promoting
international wars. I inquired whether the pronouncements
of the American Government regarding the current controversy
had not convinced the Ohinese of the friendahip of the
United States for China and the reply was that unfortunately
many Ohinese are inolined to interpret American emphasis
on Manohuria as prompted by large American financial
interests

=== Page 8 === (confidence 0.97)
2- from Shanghai,Mar.3,noon.
interests there actual or punative and to interpret
the
D.EU.
moidentelactivity of the British in trying to end
hostilities at Shanghai as prompted under those greater
interests of Great Britain in Central China.
Two. I explained to my informant that the arms businese
in the United States is entirely in private hands and that
the American Government itself purchases most of its
munitions and never sells munitions. I said I knew of
no law under which the American Government could forbid
the sale of munitions by American firms to the Japanese
Government but I thought it unlikely . so large a ale
as reported above had taken place.
Three. I do not know the origin of these malicious
rumors and absurd interpretations but if the American
Minister or the Department has any statements to make
which I might give out as coming from an authoritative
source particularly regarding thc rumored large
purchase of arms by Japan they would prove useful at
this junction."
JOHNSON

894.24/45
The Comnunist ROTEFAHNE reports today that a Japanese
oommission negotiated recently with the firm of Germania-
Reinickendorf and that this plant is now manufacturing
machine gun lock springs in large quantities. The
ROTEFAHNE further reports that the AEG cable plant at
Oberspree, which can be converted instantly for the
manufacture of munitions, already supplies field tele-
IMAR1 4 1932
phones for delivery to Shanghai and that the Fintsch works
at Fuerstenwalde manufactures mine throwers "regulators
and gun cocks". . All these plants are lccated near Berlin.
In viow of this information the ROTEFAHNE urges all
morkers to refuse work in connection with the manufacture,
transportation and shipment of war supplies intended for
imperialists. (END GRAY)
STRICTLY CONFIDENTIAL. Wiley learned yesterday from
acquaintances who are interested in a handicraft school for
workers

2-#48, from Berlin, Mar.3,5 p.m.
workers children in Halle that the Communists who are in
the majority at the nearby Leuna Works of the I. G. Farben
recently considered the question of suspending work on a
large order for poison gas ostensibly destined for Japan
but finally decided not to quit in view of the increasing
unemployment.

DIVISION OF
The Secretary of State.
.24/49
My dear Mr. Secretary:
The War Department is in receipt of communications
from business interests of the country asking if there is
objection to the sale of certain items of war materials to
Japan. These inquiries relate to supplies to be manufactured
or sold by commercial interests and not to items that belong
to the War Department.
The attitude of the State Department in regard to
the approval or disapproval of such sales is requested.
Sincerely yours
Secretary of War.

=== Page 23 === (confidence 0.98)
Mareh 25 1952.
dear Mr. Seeretary:
894.24/49
I have your letter of Maroh 8, 1932, stating that
the War Department is in receipt of comunications from
business interests in the United States inquiring whether
there is objeetion to the sale to Japan of certain items
of war materials to be manufactured or sold by commeroial
interests and not belonging to the War Department. You
request an expremsion of the attitude of this Department
in regard to the approval or disapproval of suoh sales.
In reply I may say that, as you are aware, there is
no legal prohibition against the sale by private persons
of war materials to Japan, and this Department therefore
has no legal authority to approve or to disapprove suoh
sales.
Nevertheless, the Department would appreciate being
informed of any inquiries in regard to possible sales to
any foreign country of war materials whioh, beoause of the
quantities or oharaoter of suoh prospective sales, or other
oiroumstances
The Honorable
Patriox J. Hurley,
jeoretary of war.

Seoretary of State,
Washington
Maroh 17, 1 p.m.
Following from Colonel Drysdale for War Department:
"Quiet, both fronts, Press reports to the contrary
there have been no recent inportant changes in troops
dispositions. Beginning tomorrow Japanese plan to begin
embarkation of 24th mixed brigade to be folloved by 11th
Division; the l4th Division to take over front line posi-

"DELIVERIES OF WAR MATERIAL BY BOSCH.
After we had established some weeks ago al-
ready that Bosoh had obtained extensive orders
for Japan, thus supporting the predatory war of
FILED
the imperialists and their rapacious attack on
the Soviet Union, we have learned of some new
1932
details in conneotion with the activities of Mr.
Bosch, this faithful promoter of the Hindenburg
candidature.
We are in a position to state today that
actually a large order from Tokyo has these days
been completed by the Bosch firm. It concerns a
production over a period of about l2 - 14 days,
and which was deolared and shipped to the firm
of Illies & Co., Hamburg. The order is being
shipped to the place of destination, Tokyo,
through this firm.
In this hour we appeal not only to the
Bosoh

=== Page 35 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Bosoh workers, but rather to the laborers in the
whole country to be vigilant and to thwart the
imperialistio propagandists for and advocators
of war. The capitalistio assailants of the So-
viet Union shall not even be supported by one
sorew.
The war profiteers shall not be enabled
to inorease their dividends through the blood
and lives of the laboring class at the expense
of the socialistio Soviet Union, the socialist-
1o establishment within the only state ruled by
workers. Stop the advocators of war.
PREVENT THE WAR TRANSPORTS TO JAPAN!
STRIKE WORK AGAINST THE DELIVERIES OF WAR
MATERIAL!"
"FURTHER SHIPMENTS OF AMMUNITION IN HAMBURG.
Hamburg, March 2. A truck containing nine
tons of ammunition, 7.65 and 6.35 millimeters,
has arrived in the Hamburg harbor from Karls-
ruhe (Baden). In Karlsruhe there is the German
Arms and Ammunition Faotory which was purchased
by the direotors of the Berlin-Karlsruhe Indus-
try Works after the war. Until now so-called
hunting ammunition was manufactured in this
factory.
Hamburg, Maroh 2. The rolling-mills at
Bellbrookdeioh 54 in Hamburg are engaging ad-
ditional workmen. Shell rings are being manu-
factured under high pressure and are being de-
livered to the Japanese imperialists in East
Asia.
As comrade Thälmann ascertained at a meet-
ing of workmen of the Krupp factory in Essen,
Japanese officers appeared on February ll on
the shooting-ground of the Krupp factory in or-
der to watoh the firing effeot, when the guns
were being tried. On the next day representatives
of the Kuomintang government appeared. Krupp
wants to make money everywhere.
In view of this war produotion carried on
under high pressure, the attention of workmen,
especially workmen employed in ammunition fao-
tories, must inerease tenfold and hundredfold.
Disclose

=== Page 36 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
Disclose all deliveries of war supplies to
imperialistic war criminals in the Communistic
newspapers! Refuse to transport ammunition!
Combine the struggle against the manufaoture of
war supplies with the struggle for higher wages
and better labor conditions, against exploit-
ation and Fascism!
Everything in defence of the Soviet Union!"
"BUSINESS WITH PEOPLE'S BLOOD.
Berlin, March 3. The red workers' corres-
pondents in Berlin have just succeeded in un-
veiling important details regarding the direot
support of the Japanese robbers by the gentle-
men of the Berlin metal industry. A Japanese
commission called these days at the establish-
ment of the Germania, Reinickendorf, and placed
large orders.
An extremely large number of machine-guns
is being manufactured.
The AEG Kabelwerk Oberspree which can at
any time be converted for the production of
shell-fuses and cartridges has also taken up
the bloody war business. Field telephone ap-
paratus are being manufactured and a great many
field telephone cables are shipped to Shanghai.
The Pintsch plant at Furstenwaide is manufaotur-
ing mortars for Japan. The Berlin plant of Pintsch
is producing regulators and loaders for submarine
torpedoes and shell-fuses. Also for Japan.
Our proclamation is direoted to all estab-
lishments: Take action everywhere in laborers'
meetings against the shameful greediness for pro-
fit of the German capitalists who are supporting
by their deliveries to the Japanese bandits plan-
ning to assault the Chinese workers and the So-
viet Union, the beginning of a new world massaore.
All workers: Get on your spot in the struggle
against the imperialistio war!"

MARCH 2. 1989.
Mamufecture in Hamburg of Ring Grenades for Japan.
The Walswerke (rolling mills) at No. 54 Billbrook-
deich, Hamburg, are engaging more workmen. They are
working at high pressure on mamufactures of ring grenades
for delivery to the Japanese imperialists.
At Hamburg Dock Shed No. 1 three tons of brass plates
were loaded on Monday (March lst) on a steamer destined
for Hongkong. These are war supplies for the Japanese im-
perialists now in possession of the harbor of Hongkong.
A railway car has arrived at the Hamburg docks from
Karlsruhe, Baden, with nine tons of 7.65 and 6.35 ammunit-
ion. At Karlsruhe there is the Deutsche Waffen- und
Munitionsfabrik (German Arms and Ammunition Factory) which
was taken over after the war by the Berlin-Karlsruhe In-
dustrie-Werke (Berlin and Karlaruhe Industrial Works).
Hitherto so-called hunting ammunition was mamufaotured there.
Flame and Explosive Grenades from Central Germany via
Swinemuende.
At a conference of workers of the Bitterfelder Chemie-
betrieb (Bitterfeld Chemical Works), Halle, women delegates
stated that a great part of the plant had been transformed
into an ammunition and arms factory for the Japanese imper-
ialists.
At the Wussag Works the plant is being adjusted to
manufacture war supplies. Flame and explosive grenades are
being filled there, also erplosive cartridges and star-shells.
On Pebruary 33rd, delivery was made, among other things, of
12,000 grenades to Swinemuende, where they were shipped to
Japan.
It is alloged that the firm of Stoltsenberg of Altona-
Eidelstedt (near Hamburg) has been granted permission by the
competent authorities of Schleswig to manufacture poison
gas, against which 350 working families residing there have
protested, with a reminder of the phosgene gas catastrophe
of some years ago.
MARCH 5. 1958.
450 tons of Explosives and Gunpowder being loaded at Port of
Hambur
Tin canisters destined for Shanghai and Tientsin have
been observed on the Lübeck lines, under the Klevated Rail-
way at the corner of Lippeltstrasse, Hamburg. These canis-
ters are used as containers for ammonia. The empty canis-
ters are returned from the Far Fast amd are now to be refilled
and transported to the Japanese imperialists.

450 tons of explosives and powder from the Explosives
Factory of Kruemmel, near Geesthacht (Hamburg district), are
being loaded on the S/S ETHA RICKMERS of the Rickmers Line,
at present in dook at Hamburg. The vessel is to put to sea
within 36 hours. Explosives and powder are des tined for the
Japanese imperialists.
Krupp supplies Guns.
Krupp, too, is taking a hand at supplying war material.
Comrade Thaelmann, in his memorandum on the moeting held at
Basen by Krupp workmen, states that on February 1lth a Japan-
ose military commission had studied the firing results of
guns (?) at the Krupp firing ground. On February 12th re-
presentatives of the Chine Kuomintang Government appeared for /
the same purpose. Krupp will deliver to any government.
War Supplies from the Berlin Metal Industrialists.
The Red Morker correspondents of Berlin have succeeded
in obtaining important disclosures of direct support given to
the Japanese robbers by Berlin metal industrialists. A Jap-
anese commission appeared recently at the "Germania" Works,
Reiniokendorf, and placed large orders. Machine gun locks
are being manufactured in unusually large numbers. Also the
A.E.G.Kabelwerk Oberspree (Upper Spree River General Electric-
ity Company's Cable Works), which can at any time be trans-
formed to manufacture grenades, fuses and gun armunition, is
particdpating in this sanguinary war business. Field telephone
apparatus is being manufactured, and a great many field tele-
phone cables are being delivered to Shanghai. The Pintach Works
at Fuerstenwalde are mamufacturing mine-throwers for Japanese
account. The Pintsch Works at Berlin are manufacturing regul-
ators and tensions for submarine torpedoes and grenade fuses
for Japan.
Arms and Ammunition Deliveries from Thuringia to the Japanese
Robbers.
During yesterday's (March 2nd) mass meeting held at the
Zoologiwal Hall, Comrade Duddins, district head of the Communist
Party in Thuringia, stated that the Japanese imperialists had
placed orders with the Rheinmetall-Werke (Rhine Metal Works) of
Thuringia for 250,000 grenade fuses; with the Sauer Ammunition
Factory of Suhl for several thousand carbines; with the Simson
Works of Suhl for machine guns; and with Zeiss of Jena for
field-glasses.
At a meeting of laborers of the Hamburg-American Line work-
shops, mention was made of war material transports at the Hemburg
docks. Those present disoussed the importance of from now on
keeping a lookout for such manufacturem and any use made of Ham-
burg-American Line vessels for their transportation.
MARCH 4, .......

=== Page 44 === (confidence 0.99)
MAROH 4, 1938.
War Material Deliveries from the Whole of Germany.
It is reported from Magdeburg that the firm of
Schmefer & Budenberg has recently been increasing
its output of torpedoes. Mines are being constructed
at the Eisenhuttenwerk (Iron Foundry) of Thale. At
the Magdeburg Saccharine Factory more workers have been
engaged for those sections in which erplosives are being
manufactured. The salt works are working at high pressure
on the winning of soda important for war manufactures.
Their warehouses, which hitherto were overfilled, have
now been entirely emptied.
The firm of Bosch, at Stuttgart, has received orders
from Japan, to be fulfilled in a fortnight at the latest.
Orders destined for Tokio have been placed with the firm of
Illies & Co.(importers and exporters), Hamburg.
28-Centimeter Krupp Guns for Japan via Kiel.
As the Communist organ, the "Ruhr Echo" reports, last
Monday (March 3rd) a 28-centimeter (1l.2") gun was loaded
on the train at the North Essen Station. The car was marked
destined for Kiel. The fact that during the past few weeks
a Japanese military commiesion has been observing gun firing
practice at Krupp's, Essen, leads to the assumption that this
gun shipment is also intended for the Japanese imperialists.
The Sachtleben Works, Easen, are manufacturing war chem-
icals. A testing drum has been set up in which gas tests are
being made. The workers believe that the material is intended
for the Japanese imperialists.
The Kupperbusch Works, of Gelsenkirohen, have for some
time heen manufacturing trenoh ploughs and field kitohens.
Hitherto such manufactures have been delivered mainly to India,
but now a large order is being filled for the Far East.
Last week a Japanese agent came to Krupp's and inspected
the spring manufacturing plant. He was shown how the gun springs
were used. Krupp has bought up a number of amall workshops at
Hagen, whioh are now to be employed in manufacturing gun springs.
MARCH 5. 1952.
Saltpetre from Hemburg to Japan via Calais.
Yesterday (March 4th) the S/8 ROBERT KOEPPEN was loaded
with refined saltpetre which had arrived at the Holthusen Quay
from the Wolfen I.G.Farbenfebrikem (Wolfen Industrial Dye Works)
of Bitterfeld. The shipment consisted of 15 tons. Saltpetre is
used for explosive purposes. The ROBERT KOKPPEN is destined
for Japan via Calais. Forwarders are Mesars. F.H.Hertling,
Gloekengiesserwall 25, Hemburg.

=== Page 45 === (confidence 0.98)
The Hoesch Works mamufacturing Rifle Barrels for Japan.
The "Ruhr Eoho" (Communist organ) reports feverish act-
ivities in wer mamufactures and deliveries by the Rhine and
Ruhr industrialists to the Japanese imperialists. At Rolling
Mill No. 1 steel plates are being rolled, preliminary to their
dispatch to the steel rolling works, in the form of angular,
round, T, U, and flat plates. 130 to 140 millimetre iron
quares are being rolled and cut into lengths of 25o0 milli-
metres, whereupon they are transported to the branch works at
Hohenlimburg and formed into tubes with a diameter of 62 mil-
limetres for rifle barrels destined for Japan and China. The
material is proportional steel, entered as H 960 I, Ch. S. K.
53 in the charge numbers. Such st eel can be heated to a very
high temperature.
Ruhr Industry delivers Poison Gas for Japan via Hamburg.
Recently a commission of criminal police was here to
fetch smoke and tear gas from the works. A testing drum
has been set up at the factory, in which trials are being
made. The worknen believe that it concerns a product which
1s being kept secret and is said to be intended for Japanese
receivers.
The chief raw material being manufactured at the works
is "Talgum", whioh is being exported either in solid form, as
rods, or loose in the form of powder. It is highly poisonous;
workmen handling it are attacked in a short time by severe eye
disease.
MARCH 7, 1932.
More and more Ammunition Shipments to Japan.
During the night of Saturday to Sunday (March 5 - 6th),
the S/s ISAR put to sea with 3,000 tons of ammonia on board;
the port of destination is Japan. This is undoubtedly also
a war supply.
The S/S ETHA RICKMERS took in cargo during the night of
Saturday to Sunday at Brunshausen, the well-known transhipment
place for ammunition. As almost every steamer of the Rickmers
Line is carrying ammunition and war material to the Far East,
1t may be regarded as certain that the ETHA RICKMERS has also
been loading ammunition at Brunshausen for the Far Rast. The
vessel is to put to sea to-night.
MARCH 8, 1938.
High Tide of War Manufactures at the Harburg and Billstedt
Textile Works.
(Harburg, near Hamburg)....."A fow days ago an agent from
Bielefeld came to our works. He stated that he had large orders
for Japan. This turns out to be the case. Working hours have
beem increased from 8 to 9 hours a day. Work is being pushed

..... 10 new looms have been set up and also a dressing machine

MARCH 9, 1932.
Poison Gas, Steel Plates and Aircraft Parts for Japan.
(Report from Dusseldorf under date of March 8th). We have
been informed that during the first few days of February
twenty acid drums filled with chloride were loaded at the
I.J.Leverkusen Works and forwarded to Shanghai. At the same
time we are informed that the production of sulphur has been
inoreased. As many as 16 vessels loaded with pyrites from
the Sachtleben Quarry are expected to arrive within the next
few days. (Pyrites and barytes (heavy spar) are the bases of
nitric acid (aqua fortis), gun-cotton and nitro-glycerine).
The works, which were completed in l925, were entirely
at a standatill, but have now been reopened, and 36 specialists
have been engaged. A few days ago a ahipment of "Plant pro-
tective preparations" went to Shanghai. As China is doing no
dye stuff business at present owing to the war, this can only
be intended for war material.
At the Northwest Phoenix Works, Dusseldorf, there was
recently placed an order by the Dinslaken Ammonia Works for
steel plates. The Rautenbach Company of Solingen-Mangenberg
has changed its activities to that of the production of air-
craft parts, and has already started work.
Further reports of the foregoing nature and otherwise will be
submitted as occasion may arise.

2.25 million dynamite cartridges forwarded to Japan by the
Kruemmel Company.

latter shipment of 92% dynamite cartridges is going via Reval;
the largest shipment of 450 cases via Valparaiso; and 800
cases via a third port the name of which we have as yet not
been able to ascertain. That these are unquestionably war
supplies for Japan is proved by the fact that avery one of

MARCH 11, 1932.
At the Holthusen uay, port of Hamburg, the s.s. OSTREVENT
is taking on board nitrate of sada, of which 19 carloads have

Leverkusen, March llth.- The Eunika Company, of Leverkusen-
Schlobusch, is manufacturing machines for the production of
grenades. It has already been ascertained that on these mach-
ines grenades for light field-pieces, calibre 7.5 cm., are
being turned.
At the I.G.Farbenwerke, especially in the medicinal section,
war supplies are being produced at high pressure. Working days
have been increased from five to six days. A pyrite grinder,
which had been idle for six months, was set going again. The
12 silos containing l0,000 tons of pyrites were emptied in two
shifts in a breathless hurry, although that section had been
lying idle for months. We remind you that pyrites and barites
(heavy-spar) are bases for the production of explosives. Workers,
seamen! Prevent the manufacture and transportation of arms and
war chemicals for the slaughter of the Chinese proletariat and
the imminent attack on the Soviet Union! Fulfil your revolutionary
duty!

Shipment of Ammunitions under Police Supervision.
At the Hamburg docks, 3hed 81, 60 cases of machine-

Japan Buys War Material in the United States.
(Telegran from New York, dated Maroh l4th).- Recently Japan
has been making large seltpetre purchases in the Umited
States. During the last two months 20 vessels sailed from
Hopewell, Virginia, with saltpetre for Japan. Two ships
chartered for Japan sailed from the same port for France with
large saltpetre cargoes.
Increased War Material Production at Leuna.
Since a short time ago large shipments of war materials
are being effected. l00 carloads of 40% nitrogen in sacks
are beinz shipped to Kobe and Yokohame. The filling in of the
bills of lading is being carried out with the greatest secrecy.
It is probable that the shipments will go forward via France.

MARCH 19, 1932:
German Undertakings arm Fascist States bordering on USSR.
The Werner Works, Berlin, acting on orders placed by
Japan, are constructing machines for the manufacture of
Dum-Dum bullets. Only a few weeks ago several such machines
were exported to Japan via Hamburg.
For some time past the Hirsch-Kupfer Company of Berlin
has had large orders in hand from abroad. The Eberswalde
branch of the Hirsch Works some time ago delivered to the
Fascist Lappo movement in Finland 300 million rounds of semi-
manufactured infantry amunition.
Reliable news has likewise been received that the Metall-
betrieb Loewe (Loewe Metal Works) has orders for the equipping
of a gun factory at Kovno. the Capital of Lithuania. Delivery
to Kovno via Memel is to be effected the end of March.

=== Page 56 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
The Wanderer Works manufacture War Material.
Worker correspondents report that the Manderer Works,
Chemnitz, have for some some days since been manufacturing
chassis at high pressure. It is rumored at the works that
large orders have been received and that engagement of more
personnel will be effected shortly. The worker correspond-
enta' belief that these orders cover war material deliveries,
as on March 15th a Japanese delegation inspected the factory
and had a long interview with the management, is confirmed.
MARCH 22, 1932.
Strike on the S/S "COBLENZ" in Harbor of Hamburg.- 36 Chinese
Firemen refuse to Transport War Maturial.
The fact that the s.s. COBLENZ was taking on board an
armored car, barbed wire, and other war material destined
for the Far East, aroused strong indignation aong the 36
firemen of the steamer. The crew unanimously resolved to
strike work on the veasel. Scarcely had this decision been
taken when the police appeared on the quay and, with the as-
sistance of detectives, com letely cut it of from the eneral
public. The dockers at work at Shed 73 declared themselves
in agreement with the Chinese crew in refusing to work on
war material transports. A spontaneous demonstration by over
80 dockers occurred on Saturday (March l9th) in front of the
s.s. COBLENZ. The Chinese firemen were compelled by the master
of the vessel to resume their duties.
Two Japanese Steamers take War Material on Beard.
The Japanese steamer PARIS MARU, in the Oder Dock, Hamburg,
is taking heavy goods on board. According to information sup-
plied by the dockers at work there, the heavy goods consist of
gun parts.
The Japanese steamer RAIFUKU MARU has been lying in the
Rosa Dock since March l7th, and, according to information re-
ceived, is likewise taking on board heavy goods, allegedly gun
parts.
Explosives and Guns for Japan.
Railway employee correspondents at Osnabrueck state that
at the local railway station several transports of long-bore
guns have been shunted and transported to the shooting field
at Loehningenm in Oldenburg, for shooting tests. These are
undoubtedly Krupp deliveries to Japan.
Worker cormespondents of the "Ruhr Eoho" (Communiat organ
in Ruhr Distriot) report that the a.s. FLORIDA in port at Duis-
burg is loading explosives for Japan via Antwerp or Rotterdam.
The pecking material bears Japanese characters.

=== Page 57 === (confidence 0.98)

British Arms Supplies.
(Telegram from London, March 30th).- The "Daily Worker"
reports that the Ishiakwajinia have light airplanes used
for training Japanese pilots and for observation flights,
which were constructed by the Cirrus-Hermes Engineering
Company of Great Britian.
The bombing machines of the Mitau Bishi are equipped
with Lion motors. Numerous De Haviland motors also are being
used in the Japanese army.
MARCH 23, 1932.
More Ammunition Transports via Hamburg.
Large quantities of war materials have again arrived
in Hamburg from Karlaruhe for the Japanese imperialists.
Among them are 35 cases of ammunition declared for Kobe and
Osaka. The cases bear the marks: C.F., CC-C, and B.W.I.K.
4000 Machine Gun Parts shipped from the Henckel Vorks, Solingen.
The Zwillingswerk J.H.Henckels, of Solingen, has started
on production of war material. Lust week 40o0 machine gun
parts were shipped to an aidress in Switzerland. In addition,
several railway car loads of revolver parts were delivered to
the Arms Factory at Suhl for further manufacture.
Railway Car Factory Munufatures Amunition Packing Cases.
For the last fortnight the railway car factory at Wismar
has been working at high pressure. More workmen are being en-
gaged every day. 350 worknen are now again being employed....
It is alleged that these ammunition paoking cases are being
manufactured for the Germen Reichawehr (Territorial Army), but
as no such orders have hitherto been placed, it is believed
that this material is intended for the imperia list combatants.
Japanese at the Horch Works, Zwickau.
A few days ago Japanese inspected the plant of the Horch
Works at Zwiokau. The very next day after this inspection
work was started in two shifts. A tremendous "push" was deteloped
in the work; what two men used to do must now be done in the same
time by one man. It is quite clear that the Horch Works have
received orders for war material for the Japanese imperialists.
Chemical Mamure Factory chenges over to War Chemicals.
The Grossesche Düngerfabrik (Grosse Chemical Manure Works)
at Oschersleben has changed its activities to the production of
war chemicals. For the present sales of chemical mamures are
stopped. All their chemicals were turned over to the Leuna Works.
Old chemicals which were lying about in heaps were exploded and
removed as quickly as possible. More workmen were engaged. Our
worker.

=== Page 58 === (confidence 0.97)
worker correspondents have discovered that the chemicals
at the Grosse Chemical Manure Factory are being used as
bases for poison gases.
Ammonia from Alsdorf for War Purpoaes.
The chemical section of the Annahuette (Anna Foundry)
at Alsdorf, Rhineland, has been working day and night at
high pressure for the last few weeks. Tremendous vuantit-
ies of their ammonia stocks are being shipped. Our worker
correspondents have ascertained that these shipments are
being delivered to the Far East.
Leuna Increases its War Mamufactures.
Since the visit to the Leuna Works of a military com-
mision consisting of Germn Reichswehr officors and Jap-
anese, great changes have been taking place in those sec-
tions directly connected with the production of war mater-
ials. New machines are being set going; in the gas com-
pre sor plant eight machines are now working as against
three as hitherto. At another gas works 17 generators are
going as against 13 as formerly. In this factory, at which
the bases for ammonia ore being produced, as many as six
extra generators are at work. The plant smithies cannot
cope with the amount of work; in part the workmen must work
in double shifts.

I have the honor to refer to previous despatches from
this Consulate Gemral on the subject of alleged deliveries
of war material via the port of Hamburg to the Far East, in
connection with which the following supplementary translat-

ions of articles appearing in the "Hamburger Volkszeitung"
1932
(Commpmist organ) are given:
MARCH 24 1932.
A worker correspondent reports from Gelsenkirohen: For
the last few days the Gute Hoffnungs-Hutte (Good Hope
Foundry), of Gelsenkirchen, has been working at full
eepacity. Hitherto only one shift was at work for
three days, but now three shifts are wwrking at full
eapeeity during the whole week. The foundry has an
crder for millions of meters of barbed wire, destined
for use in the imperialist war.
40.000 baga of Ammonia. - The Bottrop Coke Furnaces are
predueing large quantities of ammonia for the Japanese.
Ustil the present 40,000 begs of ammonie have been for-
warded to Hemburg vie Ruhrort.

=== Page 64 === (confidence 0.98)
The Textile Industry essiste with Equipment.
The Worsted Spinning Mills, of Leipsig, at which at
present s70 spinners are striking, are delivering yarn to
the Far Mast for the mamufacture of military overeoats.
Cloth for coats for Japanese soldiers is being woven
at Neuminster.
40,000 overcoats have allegedly been ordered of tailors
in Berlin.
The Samson Works, Lausits, recently made 10,000 military
blankets.
The Jute Factory of Harzburg has manufactured 25,000
sacks for Japan.
The Bemberg Aktien-Gesellschaft has established a new
factory in England with a staff of 700 man.
Ring Grenades from Lüdenscheid.
Kasper & Noelle, of Lädenscheid, are rolling copper
strips intended for the manufacture of ring grenades. They
nave an order for 4,000 kilograms allegedly to be forwarded
to the export firm of Hasselmeier, Rotterdam.
Rifle Bullets for France.
The Vereinigten Deutschen Metallwerken (United German
Metal Works), of Ladenscheid, is producing steel nickel
plates intended for the manufacture of rifle bullets. Also,
peroussion cap strips for cartridges are being manufactured.
The orders are going forward chiefly to France and Sweden.
The Lindem & Funker Works of Iserlohn are manufacturing
gun-carriage bolts, ammunition chests, trappings for camp-
waggons, etc. The brass foundries have been adjusted to mamu-
facture peroussion caps and parts.
MARCH 301932.
Shot-proof Glass, Gunpowder and Arsenic for Japan.
Worker correspondents inform us: - The Hell & Stahmer Company,
of Billbrook, Hamburg, is supplying Japan with war material.
Work is being carried on might and day, in three shifts.....
Gunpomder is being manufactured. Further, arsenic for foreign
shipment is being produced and also, as a recent specialty,
shet-proof glass, which probably wi ll be used for armored cars.
The factory is working at high pressure and the company has
one order after another.
Erplosive"Varre II Class."
Worker correspondents report from Libeck: Ammunition trans-
ports.....

=== Page 65 === (confidence 0.99)
ports from German armament industrialists are being for-
warded by shipowmers of all capitalist countries. A few
days ago a large transport of ammunition chests was loaded
by the Dynamite Factory of Kruemmel (Hamburg district) at
the Lübeck dooks. The s.s. REx of the Swedish Svea Line
took the cargo on board at Shed 9 last Saturday. The
chests bore the inscription : Manufacturer: Dynamite A/G
formerly Nobel A/G, Hamburg; Percussion cap Factory, Toms-
dorf; "Explosive Varre II Class". The loading of these
ammunition chests, on which the port of destination was
not marked, but whioh are undoubtedly destined for the
Japanese seat of war, took place under supervision of the
polioo.
Phosgene Factory at Ammendorf. - The Buckau Chemical Factory
at Ammendorf is making important readjustments in its plant.
Phosgene is being produced under the term of "tests".
70.000 Kilograms of Explosives. Gelsenkirchen, March 29th.-
Worker correspondents at the Dahlbusch Mine report that the
ammonia plant again has a shipment of 70,000 kilograms of
ammonia ready for the preparation of explosives, ordered by
a Japanese milisary commission. Work was done in double
shifts to expedite the order. Stocks are almost entirely
cleared.
Shell Produotion at Millheim.- Department No. 4 of the
Friedrich Wilhelm Foundry at Mullheim is manufacturing
great quantities of howitser shells, calibre l0.5. The
shells are for foreign account and are undoubtedly being
manufactured for Japan.
APRIL 1, 1932.
It is reported from Leuna: Military commissions come
to the works almost daily. Just before Master there ap-
peared a Japanese delegation, conducted by a manager of the
works, at Section M.E.l44. The commissions take particular
note of the salt and gas producing plants. The stock of
ammoniacal sulphuric acid is being delivered entirely to
Japan. Repairs at this plant are being carried out at full
pressure, which means new orders. At the generator plant a
large number of machines have started work during the last
few weeks, so thet 18 are now running. Improvements are be-
ing daily effected in the ammoniao sulphate saltpetre seotion,
whiah is likewise a sign that this part of the plant will in-
crease its output. Enormous quantities of bensine leave the
Leuna Works daily.
The Griesheim Works supply Franee and Japen.- The Griesheim
Works of Bitterfeld are continually being visited by foreign
military commissions; French commissions call especially at
the magnesium seotion. This section has for some time past
beem working at full capecity... It is rumored that a French
undertaking.....

=== Page 66 === (confidence 0.96)
undertaking is desirous of purchasing this plant. The
phosphorus stooks were completely emptied. The plant
is being repaired in great haste. Deliveries go chiefly
to France and, in part, to Japan direct. Extensive
trials are being made with fanatin, an extremely strong
metal of great resistance.
Pioneer Shovels for Japan.
Worker correspondents report that the firm of Hermann
Dahlmann, of Gevelsberg, Rhineland, is mamufacturing pion-
eer shovels at full capacity. The goods were passed through
the customs at Gevelsberg and it is rumored that this ship-
ment has gone forward to Japan.
Searchlights, Defensive Weapons against Airoraft constructed
at the Zeiss Works.
The representative of the Zeiss Works, of Jena, stress-
ed the fact that the most modern war instruments are manu-
factured by Zeiss. Japanese and Italians are continually
at the works, and especially the searchlight section under-
goes a thorough inspeotion. The visitors are greatly in-
terested in instruments of defence against airoraft.

SUDDEUTSCHE ARBEITER-ZEITUNG
Maroh 26, 1932.
Stuttgart, Germany.
Tran slation
WAR HYENAS ARE PREPARING FOR WHOLESALE SLAUGHTER
Increased War Production everywhere / Prepare for
the Anti-War Meeting!
From all parts of the Reich reports are accumulat-
ing on inoreased supplies of war equipment for the
Japanese war beaters. Imperialistic war is no more a
danger, it has become a fact. The guns and flame-cast-
ers, the bombs and shells which are being discharged
and direoted against Chinese workmen and peasants can
be used against the Soviet Union tomorrow. Every gun,
every shell, however, with which Japan is supplied is
direoted not only against the revolutionary struggle for
liberty of the Chinese people, not only against the
brothers in the Soviet nion, it is also a weapon in
the hands of German capitalists, of the Versailles op-
pressors, against the German proletariat. We are quot-
ing below the reports of one day which characterize the
seriousness of the situation.
Barbed Wire from Gelsenkirchen.
For the past few days the Gutehoffnung foundry at
Gelsenkirohen has been working to its full capacity
again. While formerly work was carried on three days a
week with one shift, it is now being carried on under
high pressure on ali week-days in three shifts. The
foundry has received an order for barbed wire amounting
to millions, which is destined for the purposes of im-
perialistic war.
40.000 Bags of Ammonia from Bottrop.
In the coke-oven at Bottrop ammonia is being pro-
duced in large quantities for the Japanese. Up to date
approximately 40,000 bags of ammonia have been shipped
to Hamburg via Ruhrort.
Textile Industry is mobilizing.
In the worsted-spinning mill at Leipzig, in which
270 spinners are now on strike, yarns are being deliver-
ed to the Far East for the manufacture of military coats.
In Neuminster cloth is being woven for coats for
Japanese

=== Page 71 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Japanese soldiers.
In Berlin orders for 40,000 military coats are to
be given to middlemen of male confection.
In the Lausitz 10,O00 military blankets were re-
cently manufactured in the Samson factory.
25,oo0 bags have been manufactured for Japan in
the Harzburg jute factory.
Shell Rings from Lüdenscheid.
Copper bands are being rolled at the factory of
Kasper and Nölle, Lüdenscheid; these bands are used
for manufaoturing shell rings. An order has been plac-
ed for 4000 kilograms which, it is said, are to be ship-
ped to the export firm of Hasselmeier, Rotterdam.
Gun Bullets for France.
In the Vereinigte Deutsche Metallwerke at Lüaen-
scheid steel nickel plates are being produced which are
used for gun bullets. In addition, percussion-cap bands
for cartridges are being manufactured. The orders have
been placed chiefly by France and Sweden.
Fuse Parts.
In the factory of Linden and Funke at Iserlohn gun-
carriage bolts, ammunition boxes and iron-work for field-
carriages are being manufactured. The brass-foundries
are manufacturing fuse-parts.
New Transports of Ammunition.
Large supplies of war material for the Japanese im-
perialists have again arrived in Hamburg from Karlsruhe.
For instance, 36 boxes of ammunition were ascertained
amongst others which have been entered at the customs
house for Kobe and Osaka. The boxes are marked: C. F.
C.-C. B. W. J. K."
Transports of war material are going to the Far East
without interruption, preparations for war, armament
against the Soviet Union are likewise being carried on
uninterruptedly. The workmen's reply to these transports
must be to refuse to manufacture war material.
In wurttemberg, too, workmen must alarm the publio
by a elose control in the factories. The Revolutionary
Trade-Union Opposition and the Communist party organize
the masses for war against the war beaters.
on

=== Page 72 === (confidence 0.93)
- 3 -
ON APRIL 3 AN INTERNATIONAL ANTI-WAR MANIFESTATION
WILL TAKE PLACE AT FRIEDRICHSHAFEN, WHICH IS TO BE
A MIGHTY DEMONSTRATION, AN EITHUSIASTIC VOW ON THE
PART OF WURTTEMBERG WORKMEN TO FIGHT AGAINST IM-
PERIALISTIC WAR.
Mobilize the factories and the workmen of the whole
of Wurttemberg for this meeting. Intensify the campaign
for the protection of the Soviet Union, fight for a free
sooialistic Germany!

DO NOT PRODUCE ANY MURDERING WEAPONS FOR
THE CAPITALISTS
Ever new Supplies of War Material for the
Imperialistic Nar Beaters.
Our workmen-correspondents renort the following,
amongst others:
Ammunition Transport in Lübeck
On Maroh 26 ammunition was loaded at shed 9 of
the Sweo Line in the Lübeck harbor onto the steamship
"Rex" which belongs to the Swedish Heavy Shipping Com-
pany. The shipment consisted of boxes marked:
"Producers Dynanite AG., formerly Nobel, Hamburg",
under which was written
"Percussion-cap factory Tomsdorf".
Another inscription read:
"Varra Explosives 2d class".
The shipment took place under police guard.
Gunpowder from Wittenberg
A commission consisting of about twenty persons
inspected the powder factory of the Westfälisch-An-
haltisohe Sprengstoff AG. at Wittenberg on Maroh 21.
Several presses which were not used since 19l8 have
now been set working again. In the section for Tulo
oil (blasting oil for powder) the number of workmen
has been increased.
Phosgene Gas Factory in Ammendorf
In the ohemical factory Buokau at Ammendorf very
important readjustments are taking place. According
to reports of workmen-correspondents, phosgene gas is
to be manufactured, the produotion of whioh is oalled
"experiments".
War Produotion in the Linke-Hoffmann Works
According to. reports reoeived by us from apprentices
of

=== Page 74 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
of the Linke-Hoffmann works at Breslau, military persons
are to be seen coming and going there almost daily.
Along with other things in which they are interested,
it is espeoially the caterpillar truoks which are manu-
factured by order of Greece and inspected by officers
of the Reichswehr. According to information received
from young workmen, these trucks can easily be turned
into small tanks.
Again 70.Oc0 Kilograms of Explosives for the
Japanese Robbers
Workmen-correspondents of the Dahlbusch mine at
Gelsenkirchen report that a shipment of 70,0o0 kilo-
grams of ammonia from the ammonia plant were prepared
for the manufaoture of explosives; this shipment was
ordered by a Japanese military commission. In order
to expedite the shipment work was carried on in double
shifts. The store-houses have been cleared almost en-
tirely.
In the Friedrich-Wilhelm foundry at Iuhlheim
large quantities of howitzer shells, caliber 10.5,
are being manufactured in section 4 of the foundry.

Ever new supplies of war material for the im-
perialistio war beaters! Imperialistic war has al-
ready become a fact in the Far East. The capitalistic
states are mobilizing feverishly for war against the
Soviet Union. Utmost vigilance and readiness to fight
are a command of the hour to all workmen.
Report on all readjustments in your factories,
which suggest war produotion, to the communistio press!
Form preparatory fighting committees for strike against
war produotion, for better wages and better labor oon-
ditions! Prevent the manufacture of arms for the im-
perialistic war beaters, for the Japanese robbers, by
means of strike!

(Tremslation of Articles from HAMBURGER VOLKSZEITUNG)
APRIL 4, 19S8.
Barbed wire for Japan.
The firm of Franz Langer, of Grüna, Saxony, is mamu-
facturing barbed wire. While there was very little work on
hand until about Christmas, an increase of produotion suddenly
set in at the beginning of this year. Work is being carried
on at high pressure in two shifts. The workmen are of the
opinion that supplies are going forward to Japan.
War Material Labor on Kaster Holidays Also.
The Emscher Lippe Mining Company, of Datteln, Westphalia,
is mending reilway carloads of ammonia to Japan. In order to
have everything ready by the date fixed, work was done even on
so-called Good Friday and during the Baster holidays. The
mineri have formed cheching committees.
Work was carried out during the holidays likewise at the
Hibernia Nitrogen Works, at Herne, Westphalia, four shifts
even being at work during Easter. They are loading "chemical
manure". Occasionally sodium nitrate is shipped. Most of these
supplies are transhipped onto vessels.
Lower Silesian Coal Mine Owners profit by War.
The Lower Silesian mine capitalists have joined the war
profiteers. They are starting with increased coke and nitrogen
deliveries. The Melchior Mine shipped thousands of tons of
coke,which had been lying there for some time, to the Skoda Works
in Csechoslovakia, which are exclusively war material mamufact-
urers. The same has been done with shipments of nitrogen. The
warehouse which a few weeks ago contained huge quantities of
nitrogen, is now empty. Similar observations have been made at
the coke furnaces of the Mirstenstein Mine, where the nitrogen
works, which a few months ago were lying idle, are again at work.
The Pieritz Nitrogen Works, of Leuna, are inoreasing their
output. Three new farnmces have started work.
ARIL 9, 1958.
Guns, Gun Parts and Fuses at the Rheinmetall Werke.
A worker correspondent reports from Sömmerda: Here at the
Rheimmetall everything is working at full strength on war sup-
plies. We have suddenly received new orders for fuses..... Al-
most lo0 men from other sections were placed in the fuse manu-
facturing mection. These mew deliveriès are certainly intended
for abread. The Rheimmetall Works are also mamufacturing guns
and gun parts. They construet all gun parts exsept the carriages
whieh are built in Dusseldorf. The gums are assembled at the
Uaterliess.......

=== Page 84 === (confidence 0.97)
-8-
Daterliess Works. A long-bore gun is also being construot-
ed and completed at these works. 150 fuse machines are at
work.
Machine Factory at Erfurt goes over to Machine Gun Construct-
For a quarter of a year the Mefurter Maschinenfabrik has
been working at high pressure. New machines are being install-
ed. Lately large quantities of pistols with take-down bar-
rels have been manufactured. Amongst the workmen it is rum-
ored that these pistols are to serve to arm the Nasis. Late-
ly the works have started to make machine gun parts. The
first machine gun plant was erected about a fortnight ago.
It is worthy of note that in those sections which chiefly
manufactured hunting rifles, the plant can quiokly be adjusted
for the manufacture of machine guns.
Arms Factory of Suhl working at full capacity.
The Arms Factory of Suhl is working at full strength.
Among others the firm of Sauer & Sohn has received an order
for over 12,000 military rifles. The workmen believe that
these rifles are for delivery to the Japanese imperialists.

"It is reported that forty laid up German vessels are to be chartered
by the Japanese for shipments ta Japan.
Confidential File

Hankow's Voluntary Report of November 18,
1932, reports that whereas Japan in normal
years required an average of about l35 long
tons of antimony regulus monthly, Chinese and
German exporters olaim that Japan is now tak-
ing more than 400 tons of antimony regulus
monthly, the objeot being the produotion of
munitions. Direot imports to Japan do not
show an inorease to this extent, but observers
believe that much of the heavy inorease in ex-
ports to so-oalled "other countries" reach
Japan via Hong Kong and other plaoes. Al-
though the world's industrial needs are lower
than ever, exports to "other countries" have
inoreased from about 27 tons monthly to about
170 tons monthly. Seoreoy in this conneotion
is probably due to a desire to avoid suspioion
and to defeat the continuing intensive Chinese
boyoott, for the Chinese refuse to sell antimony
oven indireotly to the Japanese. The Consul
General was informed, on what seems to be re-
liable authority, that a Japanese naval offioer
at Hankow attempted to purohase through a British
brokerage firm 500 tons, 200 tons of which were
sold at a figure slightly above the market
prioe, but were not delivered when the identity
of the real purohaser was learned.

=== Page 119 === (confidence 0.98)
-8-
Japan is purehasing antimony regulus in unpre-
cedently large quantities in transactions, customarily
secret, whioh have been in progress markedly since
about July, according to Chinese and German antimony
exporters at Hankow. In normal years with industry
requiring substantial amounts Japan requires an average
of about 135 long tons of antimony regulus monthly.
Both Chinese and German exporters here claim that
Japan is now taking more than 400 tons of antimony
regulus monthly. This amount, however, is not
revealed in the figures of the Chinese Maritime
Customs, but according to these exporters, it reaches
Japan through shipments via Hong Kong and other
indirect Far Eastern routes.
There is a unanimity of belief among these
exporters that the condition of industry in Japan at
the present time is such that the only reasonable
objeet for the aceumulation of heavy supplies of
antimony regulus is for the greatly inoreased
production of munitions.
Aside from the indirect shipments, the abnormal
demand of Japan at the present time is strikingly
illustrated by the visible exports of antimony regulus
to that country in July and principally in August.
This is shown in the following table of exports of
regulus to Japan, dating baok to November, l9sl, the
menth when the Chinese Maritime Customs began issuing
separate meuthly figures in a se-called "Menthly
Returas of the Foreign Trade of Chime":

It is to be noted that in each of these years
the antimony needs of industry in Japan and in

and for exports to "Other Countries" more significant.

than the figures listed herewith the exports of 169.7
long tons to "Other Countries" readily produced a
shock when it became apparent.
With their broad conneotions Hankow antimony
dealers assert that they have been able to trace a
portion of this amount in "Other Countries" to Hong

amount of antimony in the "Other Countries" group,

scattered countries, goes to Japan mainly via Hong
Kong, and possibly through other foreign Far Eastern
ports.

Mr. Li informed this Consulate General that
within the last week he succeeded in identifying
the buyer of one consignment of 2oo tons of antimony

were sold by Chinese dealers before the identity of
the buyer as the naval officer was known. He said

slightly above the market price for immediate delivery,
his own firm and another dealer involved in the
transaction immediately refused to make further
contracts in this partioular deal.

EXPORTS OF ANTIMONY REGULUS TO JAPAN
The average monthly exports of antimony regu-
lus to Japan from all China during 1929, 1950, 1931
and 1932 were 135, 135, 112 and 135 long tons, re-
spectively. While exports of antimony regulus to
Japan (and Great Britain) are continuing on the
basis of former years, despite the world-wide
depression, the exports to the United States have
dropped from 7,889 long tons in 1929 to 1860 long
tons for the first ten months of l932. The
average monthly shipments to the United States have
been as f011ows: 1929, 658; 1930, 482; 1951, 188;
1932, 186. The change in the destination of and
demand for antimony regulus has taken place in the
face of the deolining purohasing power of British
and Japanese currencies and the stable or increas-
ing purohasing power of United States ourrency.
ANTIMONY REGULUS EXPORTS TO GREAT BRITAIN
Comparing the exports of antimony regulus
from all China to Great Britain during the first
ten months of 1932 and the year 1951, 1t wil1 be
seen that the exports for the first ten months are
almost twice those during the year l9sl which seems
somemhat inoongruous in view of the reported present
poor condition of Great Britain's industries. Be-
lisble mourees state that it is possible to make
out

=== Page 130 === (confidence 0.96)
- 3-
cut export deelarations indioating the destination
as Great Britain, and without difficulty diseharge
the antimony at any Japanese port of the transport-
ing steemer's call.

DIPE ERENCE IN CUSTOMS STATISTICS AND DECLARED EXPORT
1
According to statistios (see Table I, attached)
obtained from the Customs Returns, exports of anti-
mony, all kinds, from Shanghai to the United States
during the first ten months of 1932 were 530 long
tons valued at US22,998, while the deolared export
returns of the Shanghai Consulate General show de-
olared exports of 403,200 pounds (180 long tons)
valued at $7,797. Similarly the Customs Returns
show exports of antimony from All China to the
United States during the first ten months of l932
amounting to 2,038 1ong tons valued at $92,867,
while the deolared export returns covering All
China show a total of 1,636 tons valued at 89,930.
According to dealers, goods whioh are billed to
American ports for transhipment are often carried
in Chinese Customs statistics as "shipped to the
United States.* It is possible that this may
account for the difference between Customs Returns
and the Consulate's declared exports of antimony
destined to the United States.
PURCHASES BY INDIRECT MEANS
A large Chinese dealer informed this Consulate
General's representative that he personally knew
of one shipment of antimony regulus which was pur-
chased by an American concern and by it resold to
Japanese interests. He remarked that other lots
might have been similarly turned over to Japanese
purohasers.
Another large antimony exporter said that his
oompeny

=== Page 132 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
eompany had been informed by its representative
in Europe that Japan had purchased about 400 long
tons of antimony regulus in Germany and Great
Britain, which he considered a rather unusual step.
During the last few weeks prioes of antimony
regulus have risen somewhat as will be noted from
the following quotations o.i.f. Shanghai including
export duty:
September 3, 1932, Shangha1 Taels 214.00
(US$66.88);
October 7, 1932, Shanghai Taels 235.00
(US$71.09);
November 5, 1952, Shangha1 Taels 240.00
(US$7i.40);
December 6, 1932, Shanghai Taels 245.00
(US$68.60).
As illustrative of the expense to which the
Japanese are prepared to go in order to secure
cargo they want, a Shanghai merohant informed this
Consulate General's representative that the Japanese
through indirect means recently succeeded in making
a purohase of from 5,000 to 8,000 long tons of zino
ore which was stored in or near Hankow for a long
time, after whioh a steamer from Moller & Co.,
Shangha1, was chartered, loaded with the ore and
oleared for a small port near Bremen, Germany.
After the steamer had passed Woosung, a radio was
forwarded to the captain instruoting him to proceed
to a certain Japanese port instead of to the port
shown on the ship's clearance papers. The claim
of the Japanese was that had it been knowm that
the zine ore was destined for Japan, the loading
coolies would have refused to handle the cargo.
Guarded inquiry at the Shanghai Custom House
revealed that the foregoing might well have happened,
sinee the sustoms have no control over vessels once
they

=== Page 133 === (confidence 0.96)
they have oleared port, provided the proper authority
were given by the interested parties for this change
of destination.

Dear Sirs:-

sorap to Japan.

up were purohased from the government and came from Hog Island, Phila.

is the same as this company ?
I have also been told the Sun
Co. ( Sun Oil Co.; same Co. as Sun Shipbuilding Co. ) has been selling
all kinds of oil to Japan for a long time.
This oountry is
going to war with Japan as certain as the sun rises and sets and it
dont seem right that we should put ammunitien in their gune.
Youre truly,

SUBJECT:

The attached carbon copy of a letter from the Chief
894.24/89
Quartermaster of the Canal Zone was left with me this

finished with it, together with an expression of the De-
partment's views. I have, therefore, had several copies
made for our use.
894.24
The purchase of this iron and steel scrap by the

should be the case, I doubt whether we should approve of
APR

"St. John, N.B. -- Earthquakes, not war, have been responsible

Earthquakes in recent years, the captain said, had resulted

ary of British Press Commente, March 6

Those olippings, 9 in pumber, contain
very little of interest. In the Parlia-
montary debate of March 6, inquiry was made
of the Minister for Foreign Affairs as to
whether China had paid its assessments to
the League of Nations. Sir John Simon
replied thet payments in arrears covering
the years 1922-1930 come under a special
arrangement approved by the Assembly of
the League in l930, whereby China pays
yearly on the total amount in arrears.
With respect to the contributions for 1931
and 1932, China has defaulted on about
half of these yearly assessments.
THE LONDON TIMES of March 6 describes
an attempt by members of the Anti-War
League to persuade the crew of a steamer not
imase England with a cargo of sorap iron
for Japan. The Anti-War League maintained
that this sorap iron would be used for war
purposes.
1933
An editorial from the WESTERN MERNING
NEws of Plymouth, England, upholds
Japanese position in Manchuria and
bsemres
that

that "Manohukuo" "is going to prove a fine

whioh recognize  her". This paper con-

THE MANCHESTER GUARDIAN of March 7
points out that the embargo against shipping
munitions to Japan and China having been
repealed, places Great Britain in the
unonviable position of having offended both

Dootor Wellington Koo in an article ap-

plight will be taken as a warning against

regarding the construction of new railways
in northern Manchuria. The railways men-

=== Page 193 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
(1) The Tunhua-Tumentsiang Railway

(2) The Hailung-Koshan Railway
(92 miles)
(3) The Lafa-Harbin Railway
(137 miles)
These lines represent a total of 341

writer states that the railway line from
the coast aoross Manchuria is being con-
tructed as fast as possible by the Japanese;
that the Governor Generel of Korea has an-
nounced that Rashin, on Sea of Japan, has
been selected as the port which will serve
the new railway. The port of Rashin will
be developed at a cost of 40,000,000 yen
which is only 10,000,000 yen less than the
amount spent upon the port of Dairen.

airdromes in the Japanese concessions in
the interior of Borneo and that the Dutch
offioials had refused to fall into the trap
and have rejected the Japanese offer of a
non-aggression pact.

LEAGUE OF NATIONS
CONTRIBUTIONS (CHINA).
35. SIr W. DAVISON asked the Secre-
tary of State for Foreign Affairs whether
all arrears of subscription due from the
Chinese Government to the League of
Nations have now been paid; and, if not,
what is the amount still outstanding?
Sir J. SIMON: As my hon. Friend the
Under-Secretary of State for Foreign
Affairs explained in reply to the ques-
tion of my hon. Friend the member for
Acton (Mr. Duggan) on the 4th July
last, the Chinese Government, in accord-
ance with a special arrangement
approved by the 1930 Assembly, is paying
off its arrears due for the years 1922-1930
by equal instalments over a period of 20
years, and has in both 1931 and 1932 paid
the instalments due under this arrange-
ment. Apart from these yearly pay-
ments in respect of arrears for' the years
1922 to 1930, China has also, in common
with all other States members, to make
an annual contribution towards the cur-
rent expenditure of the League. In
respect of the year 1931 China's contri-
bution was assessed at 1,438,861 gold
francs and for 1932 at 1,544,643 gold
franes. Towards these two contributions
China has paid at present 719,428 and
772,208 francs, respectively. For these
two years, therefore, China is in arrears
to the extent of 1,491,868 francs.
Sir W. DAVISON: Does my right hon.
Friend think that it is fair that China
should have the advantages of the
League and be so substantially in arrear
with her contributions, which are due
from her to the League; and can he say
whether these dues are payable by the
Chinese Government of Peking, Canton
or Nanking?
Sir J. SIMON: The Chinese Govern-
ment is not the only Government in
arrear. The Government from which the
amounts are due is the Government
which is a member of the League.
Sir W. DAVISON: Is it the Govern-
ment of Peking, Canton or Nanking?
That seems to me to be a clear question.
Mr. MAXTON: Are nations that are
in arrear allowed by the rules of the
League to take part in a war?

abroad?

Overseas Trade Department): The con-
trol exercised by this country applies
only to exports shipped from United
Kingdom ports.
84. Mr. LUNN asked the President of
the Board of Trade whether any existing
arms contract between any British firms
and the Chinese or Japanese Govern-

fulfilment of which can defeat the aims
of the arms embargo; and, if so, what
is the nature of such a clause?
Lieut.-Colonel COLVILLE: I am not
aware of the existence of any such clause.

PAPER THE TDB
DATE
CITY - JONDON
Mar. 6, 1953.
SCRAP IRON CARGO FOR
JAPAN
ATTEMPT TO PERSUADE CREW
NOT TO SAIL
The steamer Stanleyville left Blyth, North-
umberland, yesterday morning for Japan with a
cargo of scrap metal. Except for one white man
signed on as coxswain the crew are all coloured
men of various nationalities.
During the past week overtures have been made
to the crew by members of the Anti-War League.
They urged the crew not to take the vessel to sea
on the ground that her cargo of scrap metal
would be turned into munitions to fight the
Chinese. On Saturday police and shipping officials
took steps to frustrate any attempts to board the
ship by unauthorized persons. By means of a
small boat, however, two members of the Anti-
War League cluded the police guard and talked
with the crew for an hour during Saturday night.
At a meeting in Bermondsey on Saturday of
the British Anti-War Council a collection was
taken to help the crew of the Stanleyville if they
would refuse to sail. Some £105 was collected,
with promises of a further f15. Then the chair-
man announced that an offer had been made to
double what another collection would realize.
The second collection was £32.
Two delegates from the Anti-War Council.
Messrs. J. Keenan and L. Wincott, arrived at
Blyth yesterday morning, after travelling through-
out the night in a taxicab, with the money sub-
scribed at the meeting in a sack. They reached
the port just in time to see the Stanleyville far
out at sea on her way to the Far East. The
Stanleyville did not sail on Saturday on account
of bad weather.

The Embargo
What we miss in yesterday's discus-
sion of the embargo on exports of arms
to Japan and China is any evidence
that an international agreement is
being earnestly sought for a prohibition
of export to Japan alone. Only such
an agreement would be consistent with
the Report accepted by the Assembly;
if it is not reached, then a League Power
selling arms to Japan is furnishing the
aggressor with the direct means of des-
troying the viotim of aggression. Sir
John Simon intimated that, failing the
greement, we should have to
abandon our earlier decision to
export to neither Power. We
have got ourselves into a pretty
mess. When Sir John Simon announced
our policy on February 27 he called it
a "bold decision," a "very significant,
definite, and grave step," and he spoke
with great sympathy of those in whose
view "it is a horrible thing that profit
"should be made out of the supply of
"the means of provoking fAghting
"which is neither necessary nor just."
So we are now going to do the
"horrible thing" because others do it
too! The Japanese, the aggressors,
will be able to buy assistance from us,
but, knowing that it is only timidity
which has made us change our minds,
they will not love us the more for that.
The Chinese, too, will be able to buy
from us, but as, when we made the
"bold degision" to strike out a line of
our own, we deprived them, the
wronged, of the help which, before they
were deolared to be wronged, we gave
them, they will not love us either. And
in general, to take a " very signifcant,
defnite, and grave step" forward and
then, after a few days, to take is back-
ward, is not & good way to increase
one's reputation. But since Bir John
Simon on February s7 declared that his
policy sprang from "the most complete
friendliness to both China and Japan,"
it is
sort of result that might be
 is also th sord that we

JAPAN'S SECRET
JUNGLE AIR BASES
POSTS SET UP
ON WAY
TO AUSTRALIA
Matsuoka's Trap
For Holland
JAPANESE plans for a system of
disguised air bases in the Dutch
East Indies, on the route from
Japanese Formosa to Australia, lie
Mr. Matsuoka
behind the mystery of the brief visit of Mr.
Matsuoka, the chief Japanese representative at
Geneva, to The Hague.
The Japanese had offered Holland a non-aggression
pact in return for " concessions" in Dutch New Guinea.
Mr. Matsuoka would go to The Hague to work out details.
But the Dutch, whose aviators have already spotted
disguised aerodromes in Japanese concessions in the
interior of Borneo, were suspicious. They refused to fall
into the trap, the offer was declined, and the Hague visit
was cut short to one night.

JAPAN'S SEARCH
FOR ALLIES
Mr. Matsuoka's Appeal
A “VACUUM" IN THE
FAR EAST
(From our London Staf.)
FLEET STREET, WEDNESDAY.
Mr. Matsuoka, the Japanese delegate
to the League of Nations, who on
Friday sails for the United States on
the way to Japan, this afternoon put
before representatives of the British
and United States press a picture of
conditions in the Far East.
"It was with regret that Japan saw the
termination of the Anglo-Japanese
alliance in 1922," said Mr. Matsuoka,
"because in that compact Japan saw
the greatest force for the stabilisation of
conditions in the Far East. The troubles
we are now facing there may be traced
to a great extent to the breakdown of
that treaty. Is it not worth while for
the statesmen of Great Britain, the
United States, and Japan to be con-
sidering the great problem of filling up
the vacuum left by the abrogation of the
Anglo-Japanese alliance, which was the
mainstay of the Far Eastern situation
and which rendered a profound service
to the cause of peace in modern history?"
On the question of the racial make-up
of Manchuria, Mr. Matsuoka declared
that the members of the Lytton Com-
wission "Don't know what they are
talking about. Manchuris was not a
part of Chinese territory. In times past
the Chinese themselves prohibited their
countrymen from emigrating there.
FUTURE OF RUSSIA
"Russia," said Mr. Matsuoka, in reply
to & question, "will in fifteen years'
time be on the road to reconstruction
and readjustment-that  to say, in
thirty years from the dale on which the
Revelution broke out. I foresee & greater
mesidn arising out of this apparent chaos
under conditions of Boviet
he world ean feel pretty
for the next hus

BRAZILIAN OFFER TO JAPAN
FROM OUR OWN CORRESPONDENT
TOKYO, MARCH 17
The Brazilian Govèrnment is making
inquiries in regard to the construction of
30 war vessels, including cruiesrs,
destroyers, and service ships. The pay-
ment will take the form of barter in
coffee and other Brazilian products over
a period of 12 years. Shipbuilders are
withholding a decision pending fuller in-
formation. British firms have also been
approached.

ATTEMPT TO STOP SHIP
SAILING FOR JAPAN
CHARGE AGAINST A DOCKER
Following the attempt to stop the steamer
Stanleyville sailing to Japan from Blyth on Sun-
day with her cargo of scrap metal, Join Davi-
sow, a docker, of North Shields, was charged at
Blyth yesterday with being found on enclosed
premises for an unlawful purpose.
It was stated that four hours before the vessel
sailed he was seen by a police officer in the yard
of Hughes Bolckow, Limited, who were dispatch-
ing the vessel. When charged he replied: " My
party does not believe in war, and that ship is
being taken to Japan with war material. I wanted
to get on board the vessel and have a meeting
with the crew and get them to leave the ship.
Superintendent Hutchison said that he had
several officers placed round the premises as
trouble had been expected.
Davison, who stated that he was on the dole,
was remanded until to-day on £5 bail.

MR. MAPSUOKA AT THE
WAR OFFICE
BUSY DAY IN LONDON
Mr. Matsuoka, who was Japan's leading
delegate to the League of Nations until his
recent withdrawal from the Assembly,
yesterday visited the headquarters of
Imperial Chenical Industries, at Millbank,
and spent half-an-hour with the chairman,
Sir Harry McGowan. Later, with Mr.
Yoshisawa, his colleague, he visited the War
Office and reinained in conversation with
Lord Hailsham for half-an-hour.
Last night he dined with a group of City
business men, and afterwards attended a
meeting of the Nihon-Jin-Kwai (the
Japanese Club) in (Cavendish-square.
Mr. Matsuoka will leave for New York in
the Leviathan on Friday.

73D CONGRESS
