No.177
CONTRACT OF THE BOSTON IRON & METAL CO.
LETTER
FROM
THE SECRETARY OF COMMERCE
TRANSMITTING
IN RESPONSE TO SENATE RESOLUTION NO. 226, CERTAIN INFOR-
MATION CONCERNING THE CONTRACT BETWEEN THE UNITED
STATES AND THE BOSTON IRON & METAL CO., PROVIDING FOR
THE SALE OF CERTAIN MERCHANT VESSELS UNDER THE CON-
TROL OF THE FORMER SHIPPING BOARD
ApRn 26 (calendar day, My 2), 1934.—Ordered to lie on the table and to be
printed
DEPARTMENT OFCOMMERCE,
OFFICE OF THE SECRETARY,
Washington, May 1, 1934.
The PRESIDENT OF THE SENATE,
Washington, D.C.
Sir: In response to Senate Resolution 226, adopted April 26 (cal-
endar day, April 27) 1934, reference is made to my letter of April 12,
1934 (S. "Doc. 169), in which, pursuant to Senate Resolution 221, I
set forth at some length the circumstances concerning the contract
with Boston Iron & Metal Co., dated November 5, 1932, providing
for the sale and scrapping of certain merchant vessels then controlled
by the former Shipping Board.
Replying to the several specific inquiries set forth in Senate Resolu-
tion 226, I repeat that on October 13, 1933, I formally notified Boston
Iron & Metal Co. that the United States withdrew from sale and
delivery all vessels covered by said contract that theretofore had not
been delivered. My action at that time was predicated upon article
third of the contract, which reserved the right to the United States

=== Page 220 === (confidence 0.99)
2
CONTRACT OF THE BOSTON IRON & METAL CO.
to withdraw from sale for scrapping any vessel or vessels the United
States desired for operation or for sale for operation.
At the time I directed that no further deliveries of vessels be made,
it sufficiently appeared that the United States might immediately
require the undelivered vessels for operation in the commerce of the
United States and for the purposes set forth in section 1, Merchant
Marine Act, 1920. 1 do not think it would be compatible with the
public interest to set forth any further information at this time rela-
tive to the Government's retention of the undelivered vessels.
Meanwhile, and as stated in my letter to the Senate dated April 12.
1934, the question as to the legality of the alleged contract with
Boston Iron & Metal Co. has been given attention, both by this
Department and by the Department of Justice. Under date of April
19, 1934, the Attorney General has rendered a written opinion (copy
attached) in which he holds that the Merchant Marine Act, 1920.
does not confer authority to sell vessels for scrapping which have an
appraised world-market v alue greatty in excess of their serap value,
and the Attorney General also states:
Nor do I find any authority outside of the Merchant Marine Act enabling you
to sell such vessels for serap.
As reported to the Senate in my previous communication, it has
been disclosed that the only appraisement of the 124 vessels covered
by the Boston Iron & Metal Co. transaction was made on September
14, 1923. Thereafter, the former Shipping Board yearly applied an
arbitrary depreciation charge to all the vessels, irrespective of their
individual value. On this basis, the records show that the 124 ves-
sels in question were carried at an operation or sale valuation of
$11,473, 443 as of July 1, 1932. This figure was supposed to represent
the " as is value of the vessels after a deduetion had been applied
to cover cost of estimated repairs necessary to place the vessels in
g d condition for operation. Under the accepted offer of Boston
Iron & Metal Co., the U'nited States was to receive approximately
only $55s,000 as to the tonnage serap value of the 124 vessels.
Applying the recent opinion of the Attorney General to the facts,
it clearly appears that the at tempted sale of these vessels to Boston
Iron & Metal Co. was unauthorized and that the supposed contract
of November 5, 1932, was illegal and void.
Congress has not spoken as to the disposition of the Government's
merchant vessels as scrap which have an operating value greatly in
excess of their scrap value, and the Department would welcome
additional and clarifying legislation on this point, as suggested in the
Attorney General's opinion of April 19, 1934. To illustrate the
importance of some decision on the part of Congress, I submit the
following information as to the Government's merchant vessels in
possession of the Depart ment:
At the present time, the Shipping Board Bureau has a total of 277
vessels, including the 86 ships undelivered to the Boston Iron & Metal
Co. for serapping. Out of the 277 vessels now held by the Bureau,
228 are in the laid-up fleet and 46 are being operated under agree-
ment with private operators. A joint committee, representing the
Navy Department, the War Department, and the Shipping Board
Bureau, has recently made a report on the 277 vessels and recom-
mends that 169 of the 277 vessels be held available as naval auxiliaries

=== Page 221 === (confidence 0.99)
CONTRACT OF THE BOSTON IRON & METAL CO.
3
and that the remaining 108 vessels, having no naval defense value,
be subject to disposal.
The question still remaining is as to what should be done with the
remaining 108 vessels. The real difficulty arises in relation to those
vessels which have an "as-is" world market value greatly in excess
of their junk value, but, if these vessels were sold today, their oper-
ation would further depress the shipping industry, in which there
is now considerable excess tonnage which must remain idle.
Sincerely yours,
DANIEL C. ROPER,
Secretary of Commerce.
DEPARTMENT OF JUSTICE,
OFFICE OF THE ATTORNEY GENERAL,
Washington, D.C., April 19, 1934.
The SECRETARY OF COMMERCE.
My DEAR MR. SEcRETaRy: I now have the honor to comply with
your recent request for my opinion concerning the proper exercise of
the powers transferred to you by section 12 of the Executive order of
June 10, 1933, which were originally vested in the United States
Shipping Board by the following sections of the Merchant Marine
Act of 1920 (41 Stat. 98s, 990: U.S.C., title 46, sec. S61, S64):
Sec. 1. That it is necessary for the national defense and for the proper growth
of its foreign and domestie commerce that the U'nited States shall have a mer-
chant marine of the best equipped and most suitable types of vessels sufficient
to carry the greater portion of its commeree and serve as a naval or military auxii-
iary in time of war or national emergeney, ultimately to be owned and operated
privately by citizens of the United States; and it is hereby declared to be the
poliey of the United States to do whatever may be necessary to develop and en-
courage the maintenance of such a merchant marine, and, in so far as may not be
inconsistent with the express provisions of this Aet. the United States Shipping
Board shall, in the disposition of vessels and shipping property as hereinafter
provided, in the making of rules and regulations, and in the administration of the
shipping laws keep always in view thi- purpose and object as the primary end to
be attained.
Sec. 5. That in order to accomplish the declared parposes of this Act, and
to carry out the poliey declared in seetion 1 hereof, the board is authorized and
directed to sell, as soon as practicable, consistent with good business methods
and the objects and purposes to be attained by this Act, at publie or private
competitive sale after appraisement and due advertisement, to persons who are
citizens of the United States except as provided in section 6 of this Act, all of
the vessels referred to in seetion 4 of this Act or otherwise acquired by the Board.
Such sale shall be made at such prices and on such terms and conditions as the
Board may preseribe, but the completion of the payment of the purchase price
and interest shall not be deferred more than fifteen years after the making of the
contract of sale. The Board in fixing or accepting the sale price of such vessels
shall take into consideration the prevailing domestic and foreign market price
of, the available supply of, and the demand for vessels, existing freight rates and
prospects of their maintenance, the cost of constructing vessels of similar types
under prevailing conditions, as well as the cost of the construction or purchase
price of the vessels to be sold, and any other facts or conditions that would in-
fluence a prudent, solvent business man in the sale of similar vessels or property
whieh he is not foreed to sell. All sales made under the authority of this Act
shall be subjeet to the limitations and restrictions of section 9 of the Shipping
Act, 1916 as amended.
The nature of your inquiry is indicated by the following paragraph
of your letter:
The Department has at the present time a number of vessels which seetion 5
of the act above referred to imposes upon me the duty to sell, that have an ap-
praised world market operating value greatly in excess of their serap value, and

=== Page 222 === (confidence 0.99)
4
CONTRACT OF THE BOSTON IRON & METAL CO.
which, with necessary repairs, could be made serviceable for operation. In
selling these vessels, however, I am anxious to avoid anything that would work
to the disadvantage of the merchant marine or in any way affect its welfare. I
accordingly have the honor to request your opinion as to whether I have the
power or authority under the act above referred to, or any other law, to sell
vessels for scrapping which have an appraised world market operating value
greatly in excess of their serap value, the advertisement for bids to specify that
such vessels are to be used for no purpose but serapping.
As the question is thus stated by you, I am of opinion that it
should be answered in the negative.
Attorney General Sargent (34 Op. 525) thought the provision
authorizing the Board to prescribe the conditions of the sale empow-
ered it to require that the ships be scrapped and, therefore, to au-
thorize a sale for scrapping. The particular sentence reads:
Such sale shall be made at such prices and on such terms and conditions as the
Board may prescribe, but the completion of the payment of the purchase price
and interest shall not be deferred more than 15 years after the making of the con-
tract of sale.
I think it is more logical to assume that the word "conditions"
is to be understood as having some relation to the other words of the
same sentence concerning prices, terms, and deferred payments, con-
sidering the ships as operating units, and in view of the poliey declared
in the first section of the statute, above quoted, rather than as con-
templating the imposition of a condition which may be totally unre-
lated to these factors and of such nature as to destroy the value of
the ships as ships that is, as operating units.
I fully appreciate that there may be circumstances in which it
would be of doubtful expediency to sell particular ships for operation,
or to dispose of a large number for that purpose, especially at a given
time, having in mind the declared policy of the statute. These con-
siderations, however, cannot add to the statute empowering language
which it does not contain, and cannot, therefore, supply the authority
to sell as scrap vessels which have an operating value greatly in ex-
cess of their scrap value. Nor do I find any authority outside of the
Merchant Marine Act enabling you to sell such vessels as scrap.
It is, therefore, my opinion that you are without authority to sell
the ships for scrapping under the circumstances stated. If, however,
you deem it expedient that they should be so disposed of as a matter
of policy, at their scrap value, I suggest the advisability of submitting
the matter to Congress for additional and clarifying legislation.
Respectfully,
Homer CumMINGS,
Attorney General.

I have the honor to refer to the Consuli te's
by Japan of Ceylon plumbago, and to enclose herewith
a table showing the exports of plumbago from Ceylon
for the first quarter of each of the years 1930-1934.
The table enclosed rather qualifies the state-
ments made in the despatches referred to and while
shipments of plumbago to Japan have certainly in-
JUN 15
creased, the increase, comparatively, is not so alarm-
PHL.D
ing.
Japan's share of the total trade has risen in
1934
five years from 11.26% to 34.01%, the present share,
and a peak of 38.48% was reached in the first quarter
of 1933. The total exports for 1933 show that Japan
took alightly more than 37% while the United States
purchased 23%.
The figures for the first quarter of
 the present year, instead of indicating that exports
to Japan are increasing, show that the United States
now

=== Page 231 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
now takes 28.88% and Japan but 34.01%.
The figures presented in the enolosed table
show that the increase of shipments to France is
even more startling than the increased exports to
Japan inasmuch as France, in 1930, purchased but
.98% and now purchases 4.36%.
The total exports
in the first quarter of l930 were slightly greater
than in the same period of the present year.
Com-
paring 1930 and 1934, France increased its importa-
tions of Ceylon plumbago by over 400% while Japanese
importations show an increase of less than 300%.
Comparing 1933 and 1934, the Umited States in-
creased its share of the exports of Ceylon plumbago
from 12.89% to 28.88%, while Japanese importations
declined from 38.48% to 34.01%.
These differences
should be borne in mind, especially, as the total
exports show an increase from 43,432 to 62,978 hun-
dredweight of ll2 pounds each.
The average price of Ceylon plumbago has in-
creased from Rupees 6/60 per hundredweight for the
first quarter of 1933 to Rupees 6/93 per hundredweight
in the same period of the present year.
An interesting note with regard to the importa-
tions into Japan of Ceylon plumbago was made in the
Annual Report of the Ceylon Chamber of Commerce. It
was stated that it is understood that Japan uses Cey-
lon plumbago for the manufacture of crucibles which
are needed for smelting high-class steel and so far
no

=== Page 232 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
no Madagasoar plumbago has found its way to Japan.
Another interesting note, considering the sta-
tistios for the present year, was the statement that
"the l0% preference in the United Kingdom has dis-
tinotly helped the trade of Ceylon plumbago with the
United Kingdom, whioh has increased from 1,192 to
1,479 tons, a distinot improvement on her purchases
for the previous three years."
The statistics for
the first quarter of 1934 show, however, that ex-
ports to the United Kingdom have declined from 10,495
to 5,859 hundredweight or that the United Kingdom now
takes 9.3% as compared with 24.16% in the same period
of last year.
. This would seem to indicate that Im-
perial Preference is not maintaining the advantage it
originally offered or, on the other hand, it may indi-
cate that the higher price of Ceylon plumbago prevents
competition with the products of other producing areas.
While the statistics which are presented with
this despatch may not be valuable to the Government,
they indicate that a careful analysis of statistics
frequently alters first-hand conclusions.
In the
present case, the alarm at increased shipments to Japan
might well be turned toward the proportionately greater
increase in exports to France.
However, it is probab-
ly safer to conclude that any increase, where such exists,
is the result, not of preparations for war, but of a
general

=== Page 233 === (confidence 0.95)
goneral improvement in trade conditions throughout
the world.

Moedlelet himigsline

I am sorry that your little memorandum to
this Division, which accompanied this letter
of May 39 from Mr. LaVarre when it first

my efforts to get previous papers from the
Index Bureau. In consequence of my second
call, that of June 13, most of the requested

port from the Consul in Trinidad which has
not been attached. As I recall, it was based
upon information communioated to the Consul

the neighborhood of the Japanese colonies
in the Amazon Basin. I wonder whether it

formant appeared to believe that the Japanese

the real purpose of the Japanese appeared to
be to get a iarge number of their people into

I have the honor to refer to my despatch
FILED
No. 865 of December 7, 1934, and to point out that
the countries whose purchases of Uruguayan products
in the form of meats, wool, and hides have received
the greatest publicity in the Uruguayan press in the
last half of 1934 have been Germany, Italy, and Japan.
Germany's purohases of wool and hides have been report-
ed to the Department, both by the Legation and by the
Consulate General. Mention has been made of Italian
purohases

=== Page 262 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
purchases of meat in my despatch No. 738 of Septem-
ber 6, 1934.
It appears now that there exists possibility of
Japanese purchases of Uruguayan meat, wool, and hides
regarding wh ich meetings between the Japanese commercial
delegates now in Montevideo and Uruguayan exporters
have been held at the Ministry for Foreign Affairs.
It seems impossible to avoid noting that the coun-
tries which have lately been engaged in purchasing the
raw commodities ordinarily exported from Uruguay are
the countries in .hich military preparations may be
stated to have progressed far in excess of the require-
ments of defense.
I have learned through the Spanish Commercial
Delegation lately negotiating with Uruguay that in
spite of Uruguayan insistence, the Spanish negotiators
have refused to make purchases of Uruguayan meats which
633.5231
they claim were not needed by the Spanish population.
It seems, therefore, not unreasonable to attribute some
significance to the type of purchases made in Uruguay
by countries in which the arming of the population seems
to have progressed in a disturbing manner.
Respectfully yours,
Leon Dominian

Despatch, dated March 19, 1935, from
Guaymas.
Because of labor troubles and poor
facilities, a plan to scrap a number of
old vessels for shipment to Japan has
been abandoned. A shipment of scrap
iron which recently left Guaymas for
Japan is understood to have been a
financial loss to the shippers.

APH 10 1935
the dimmantling of three old Mexican ships in the Port

of Guaymas for the purpose of shipping the sorap-iron

LIONEL left Guaymas a few days ago with approximately
6,0oo tons of sorap-iron for Japan. The LIONEL
oleared from this port for Los Angeles, California,
whence it is to proceed to Japan.
Three old Mexican Vessels, the WASHINGTON, BOLIVAR,
and MOCTEZUMA, were towed from Topolobampo to Guaymas
by a Mexican coast guard vessel and work was begun
several months ago on preparing the metal for shipment to
Japen.

=== Page 277 === (confidence 0.97)
● 8 -
Japan. It was reported in the looal press that Messrs.
Ness and Dundiseh, who represented a wreoking oompany of
Mexioo City known as "Aga", had bought a number of old
vessels in the United States with a view to bringing
them to Guaymas to be dismantled. The sorap-iron and
other metal was to be shipped to Japan. Suoh a plan
appears to have been abandoned as impracticable. In
fact, it is understood that because of labor troubles,
lack of machinery and faoilities, et cetera, the process
of obtaining metal from the dismantled vessels proved
to be very costly and severe losses were suffered by
the promoters of the undertaking.
In this conneotion,
24
please see my despatoh No. 99, of Maroh 15, 1935,

because of the conditions just mentioned and had not the
shops of the Southern Pacifio Railway of Mexico been so
near with a considerable quantity of sorap-iron available,
the vessel probably would have left Guaymas with only
about 9o0 tons of metal. The railway sold Messrs. Ness
and Dundisoh 2,200 tons of sorap-iron (including locomo-
tive wheels, axles, rails, and other first class material)
at 24.50 pesos per ton (1 peso equals approximately $0.28)
and 2,s00 tons of so-called second grade sorap-iron at
21 pesos per ton whioh, with the freight and other expenses
ineurred, amounted to a total of 124,500 pesos. While no
definite information is available as to the price at which
this sorap-iron was sold in Japan, it is said that the
first grade material was sold at Us$ll.50 per ton and the
balance at Us$8.00 per ton, f.o.b. a Japanese port.

has been abandoned.

ORIGINAL TELEGRAM SDNT TO WAR DEPARTOENT.
(COPY)
TELEGRAM
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
LWU MO 251 NL 5:47 a. m.
Norwalk, Conn. April
25, 1935
DISCN OF
AN AFFAIRS
The President:
It has been reported to me that representatives from
Japan are negotiating for an exclusive license for Japan, China
and Manchuko under a newly perfected process for the solififica-
tion of liquid hydrocarbons such as gasoline, kerosene, lubrica-
ting and fuel oils also ether and glycerine. The cost of solidi-
fication is less than one cent per gallon. In their solidified
forms they are non-explosive and are not affected by temperature
8
extremes, there is no appreciable vaporation loss and they may
394.24/126
be quickly and inexpensively reliquified under pressure. After
reliquidification a chemical analysis will be practically identical
with one made before solidification. The solidified materials
are especially adaptable for storage purposes which might prove
of interest in time of war to use for heat, light, or power units.
I personally have certain American commercial rights and through
this connection learned of these negotiations. My patriotism may
have made me unduly apprehensive but if you consider the possible
9
hazard or benefit worth investigating I shall be very glad to be
of service in any way possible. Time is a vital element as I
believe the contact man is to leave New York April 30th. Since
I am unknown to you for any personal information about me which
you may desire I am glad to refer you to Louis Brown, president
Johns Manville Corporation New York City, Myron Clark, executive
vice president Reading Iron Co., Reading, Pa., A. G. Cameron,
president Goodyear Export Corpn., Akron, O., Marcia C. Hamblet,
Wilson Point, South Norwalk, Conn.
FILED
co
F
G

were involved, a heavy movement of scrap

into tens of thousan ds of tons daily,

=== Page 298 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
November 6, 1935.
Mr. Hamilton telephoned Mr. Harding,
Chier of the Metals and Minerals Division
of the Department of Commerce, in regard
to the attached telegram from Congressman
Disney. Mr. Harding stated that so far
as he knew the Japanese Government main-
tains no official agent in the United
States for the purchase of scrap steel but
that the Department of Commeroe's records
indicated that a number of firms -- Mr.
Harding mentioned two Japanese concerns --
purchased scrap steel in the United States
for shipment to Japan. Mr. Harding added
that under the Department of Commerce
regulations they did not ordinarily give
out the names of such purchasers; that they
might modify their rule in the case of a
Congressman but that the matter would require
decision by some of the higher officials in
the Department of Commeroe. Mr. Harding
thought that the most expeditious and most
effeotive manner of handling Congressman
Disney's inquiry would be for this Department
to telegraph the Congressman suggesting that
he address his inquiry to the Japanese Consul
General in New York City. Mr. Hamilton
stated that this Department would probably
follow that procedure unless other officers
in the Department thought that it would be
preferable that this Department refer the
matter to the Department of Commerce to
handle.
Subsequently

=== Page 299 === (confidence 0.93)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
一8 -
Subsequently, Mr. Harding telephoned
Mr. Hamilton and stated that in thinking the
matter over he (Mr. Harding) thought that the
best course of action might be to give the
Congressman a direot answer to the effect
that the leading commercial buyers in the
United States are Mitsubishi Trading Company,
180 Broadway, New York City, and Mitsui and
Company, 65 Broadway, New York City.

CONFIDENPTLAL
SHIPMENT OF SULPHATE OF AMMONIA TO JAPAN.
IP The
From:
Milton Patterson Thompson,
American Vice Consul.
American Consulate,
Date of Preparation: January 20,1936.
Matanzas, Cuba.
Date of Mailing:
January 2,1936.
FEB 18 1936
FILED
869.2/869.4
MPT/Jo.
Distribution:
Original and four copies to the Department;
One copy to the Commercial Attaché, Habana;
One copy to the Supervising Consulate General,Habana.

=== Page 309 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
The SS. NORFOLK MARU, (Japanese) of the "K" Line,
owned by the Kawasaki Steamship Company, sailed from
Matanzas, Cuba, on January 17, 1936, for Japan, via
New Orleans, Louisiana, and the Panama Canal, with a
cargo of 2,7oo long tons of sulphate of ammonia, valued
at $76,000, bought from Armour and Company (American)
and shipped by its local fertilizer plant, which order
was consigned to Nitsuhishi Company, Limited. It is
understood that 1,ooo tons are to be discharged in
Kobe, while the remaining 1,700 tons are destined for
Yokohoma.
Nature of Transaction.
This order represents a three-cornered transaction,
involving barter and cash. The sulphate was acquired
in the first instance by Armour and Company in Germany,
where it was produced synthetically, in the nature of
payment in kind for foodstuffs sold to Germany, the
credits for which, under the Reich's regulations, could
not be exported therefrom in currency. The consideration
in the deal between Armour and Company and the Japanese
purchaser is cash. It is expeoted that additional sales
of sulphate of ammonia acquired similarly by Armour and
Company will be made in the near future to Japanese
interests.
Cuban Aspect of Shipment.
This shipment is a re-export from Cuba, the cash
received by the seller being paid directly to the parent
office in the United States of Armour and Company, con-
sequently, the only benefits received by Cuba incident
to

=== Page 310 === (confidence 0.97)
- 3 -
to the sale were the expenses of unloading and load-
ing the sulphate, the import duty (of $0.88 a ton)
paid, and the taxes paid by the ships. It is estimated
that about 2,ooo was paid to local stevedores for the
loading in the outward movement of this chemical.
It is not improbable, though no definite evidence
to that effect is available, that the Japanese desire to
increase the volume of their purchases from Cuba to
entitle them to a more favorable classification under
the Cuban import tariff.
STRICTLY

=== Page 311 === (confidence 0.98)
-4 -
STRICTLY
CONFIDENTIAL
Use of Shipment.
The manager of the Matanzas' fertilizer plant of
Armour and Company, which shipped the sulphate of ammonia,
is under the impression that the use to which this chemical
is to be put is the manufacture of fertilizer by the
Nipponese. However, apropos of this thought, it is pointed
out that sulphate of ammonia is susceptible of use in the
manufacture of munitions, for which purpose the German
plant which fabricated this particular shipment was erected
during the World War. Furthermore, it is possibly not
devoid of significance that the carrying ship of this
order to Japan is proceeding to New Orleans from Matanzas
to take on a cargo of cotton, whioh is likewise used in
the making of munitions.
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 312 === (confidence 0.98)
-5-
CONFIDENTIAL.

The Manager, Fertilizer Plant, Armour and
Company, Matanzas, Cuba.
The Master, SS. NORFOLk MARU.
The writer's observations and knowledge.

JOHN LHE BYRNES

Note
March 22, 1936.
Honorable Stephen Early,
894.6363
The White House,
Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. Early:
I would much rather take the plane for Washington
and then oall you on the phone for an appointmont and
have a personal ohat on the subjeot uppermost on my
mind; however I know how crowded you are for time so have
deeided to talk with you this way and when you find a
convenient time to do so, you may read this at your
894.24/140
leisure, and I will be very grateful to you for doing so.
For thirteen years I have been in the oil produetion
business here and was substantially successful in it
until -- well you know what happened for you helped to
get us all out or it. For some I have been planning to
get back into the business again and have been quietly
figuring things out so as to get in to it in a big way.
No matter how I figured and planned I could not get by
the obstacle that wrecks all independent operations, the
Standard Oil Co. Then came a lucky break and a great
idea through my very good friend, Colonel Henry Jewett
Furber who introduced me to Mr. Kazuo Takahashi, head of
the N.Y.K. Line in the United States.
From several conversations and meetings the idea
occurred to me that if a satisfactory plan could be work-
ed out I could get the oil business of Japan. Here it
is as far as it has progressed: they consume a tremendous
amount of oil most all of whioh they purohase from the
major companies, but while this all fine in peace times
there is considerable diffieulty if say they should have
war with Russia. Then, too, petroleum and its produots
are costing Japan more than it is economical for them to
pay for it and there are important angles involved. I
am proposing to them that I organize an oil company to
produce and refine the oil, they will finanee it by a
bond issue but it is to be operated and eontrolled by my
produetion to the limit that they can absorb.
▲t

=== Page 322 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
At the same time we organize a company in Mexieo te
which the oil 1s delivered and by whom it is refined.
From there say Ensenada it is delivered to the tankers
and on to Japan.
Now in addition to the interest on the bonds they
are to receive 25 pereent of the net profits of the
corporation, the operators 25 percent and the other 50
peroent for operating expenses, eto., to a specified
limit above whioh that to be distributed as above pro-
portions. What crude and refined produots they do not
absorb will be disposed of through the domestic markets
at the prevailing market prices.
I am in communication with Mexioo City regarding an
available site at the harbor of Ensenada and sufficient
land for ample storage and refinery. Further I have in
mind and have suggested to them that the company make a
deal with the Government of Mexico to explore for and
develop known potential oil lands on a firty fifty basis
or in other words a cooperative plan. Have suggested that
they could have a representative on the board of the
Mexioan company.
The Mexican Consul appears to be much interested in
the plan and I feel that Mexico City should be also, for
it would be the first time suoh a proposal has been made
to them and it would be very profitable for them. I am a-
waiting word from them with great interest.
In olosing, this plan would only operate during
friendly relations between the United States and Japan, its
resouroes then to be immediately available to our own
country.
I would deeply appreoiate your opinion of this plan,
which I pledge you, would be kept striotly confidential;
and if there is possibility of objeotions to it from
Washington.
Your comments will be most welcome and awaited with
great interest.
With sincere regards, I am,
Cordially Yours,
JOHN L. BYRNES.

AND RECCRDS
Mr. Edward Farley, Trustee in Receivership for the
Munson Line, telephoned to say that the Pacific Coast Line
is offered a contract for the carriage of a cargo of scrap
iron to Japan towards the end of August. He said he
894.24/152
wished to consult me to get any possible light on the
way in which the transaction might be affected by Govern-
ment action, for if the transaction was not carried out,
the line could be sued for loss if the price of scrap
iron rose.
I told him that the Department, as a matter of
regular policy, never gave advice on such questions involv-
ing business risks. I explained, to correct a somewhat
hazy idea he had of the neutrality legislation, that even
should a neutrality proclamation be issued by the President
under Seotion 1 of the Act, Section 2(a), having to do
F
with shipment on American vessels, would not automatically
come into operation; a separate proclamation would be requred.
At the conclusion of the conversation he thanked me
for the information and stated he thought it would be posg
sible to work out some "hedge" olause which would proteot them.

DIVISION OI

FAR EASTERNALAIRS
NP F6 837
1937
OTED
Mr. Edgar Smith, representing the General Motors
Corporation, called at my office this morning. He said
that his corporation wished to abide fully by any policy
894.24/158
which the Government might have in respect to exports to
Japan during the present conflict in the Far East. He
explained that his corporation was exporting ten thousand
cars annually to Japan and that the great majority of
these cars were trucks which might be used for military
purposes. He said that the number ten thousand was the
quota fixed by the Japanese Government, and that h1s
F/MR
corporation thought it possible that the Japanese might
increase that quota in the near future.
L0
I told Mr. Smith that no announcement had been made
by the Government of any policy relating to restrictions
0
on exports to Japan, but that we were on a 24 hour bas
watohing carefully all developments in the conflict in
the Far East, and that he should bear in mind the possibil-
ity that the President might find it incumbent upon him
to

=== Page 356 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
to issue a proclamation under the Neutrality Act in respect
to China and Japan, and that the Government might find it
necessary to formulate some restrictive policy in respect
to exports to those two countries in addition to the re-
striotions which would become legally effective under
proclamations pursuant to the Neutrality Act. I added
that unless or until such action as I had indicated had
been taken, it seemed to me that American compan1es could
govern their actions in respect to exportation to China
and Japan in accordance with their own ideas of their
best interests.
Mr. Smith said that his corporation felt such
repugnance to the sale of trucks to Japan for probable
military use that it was seriously considering refusing
to sell any more during the present conflict whether this
Government expressed objection to such exports or not, and
that it would certainly refuse to sell trucks to Japan
at this time if its competitors were willing to enter
into a self-denying agreement with it.
Joseph C. Green.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE

September 10, 1937.
894.24/1
Mr. Smith also called on me (on Septem-
ber 8) and made to me the same statement, in
effect, that he made to Mr. Green as recorded
in the memorandum hereunder.
I said to Mr. Smith that it is clear from
the published record that this Governnent
deplores the conflict which is going on in the
5
Far East, would gladly see it ended, and is
6
very solicitous that this country not become
involved; also, that it is evident that the
people of this country do not wish that this
country contribute to the waging and the pro-
longation of armed conflicts. I said that if
enough individuals or firms woula make it
clear that they are willing to forego profits
and to refrain from trade with countries
inclined toward or engaging in use of force,
F/MR
it would soon follow that governments would
be in position to apply processes which would
render the waging of wars more difficult; and
that, expressing a personal view and speaking
not officially, it seemed to me that large
firms and powerful corporations could set a
very good example in such matters if they felt
inclined voluntarily to do so.
Mr. Smith said that he thought that in
the long run General Motors might reap edvan-
tages from taking a helpful stand in such a
matter.

UNITED STATES EXPORTS TO JAPAN AND CHINA
894.24/162
DURING 1937
I. Movement of Total Exports.
The value of United States exports to Japan and
China during the first three quarters of the ourrent
894.24
year has been on a much higher level then during the
corresponding months of 1936, although the increase
2
has been greater in relation to Japan than to China.
893.
As shown in Table I, United States total exports to
Japan up to the end of September were 70.3 peroent
greater than during the first nine months of 1936.
Average monthly exports of 24.25 million dollars dur
ing the first half of l937 were nearly one and one
half times as large as the monthly average for the
calendar years 1935 and 1936. They were st1ll larger
in the first two months of hostilities with China.
The smaller figures of 16.8 million for September and
21.1 million for October are more than accounted for
by the marked dropping-off in United States cotton
exports. (see below, section II.)
Exports

=== Page 370 === (confidence 0.98)
-8
Exports to Kwantung Leased Territory (destined for
Manohukuo) during the first three quarters of 1937 were
about one third larger than for the corresponding period
of 1936. Shipments 1n September exceeding 3 million
dollars were ten times above the monthly average for l936.
A smaller rate of increase is shown in United States
exports to China and Hong Kong, which, for the first nine
months of 1937, were respectively 28.9 percent and 23
peroent above the corresponding period for 1936. Ship-
mento to China reached their highest point in August,
but with the tightening of the Japanese blockade and
intensification of war risks, exports to Chinese ports
were reduced in September to about one quarter of the
August figure, and this includes exports for North
China ports now under Japanese control. On the other
hand, American exports to Hong Kong show a sustained
increase, and whereas in the first half of 1937 the
value of such was less than one seventh of that to
the Chinese Republio, shipments to Hong Kong were
actually grcater in September than to China proper.
II. Changes in Commodity Composition of United States
Exporta.
Table II shows the changes in quantitative terms of
prinoipal United States exports to Japan and China.
Prinoipal

=== Page 371 === (confidence 0.98)
30
Prinoipal Exports to Japan
For the January-September period of the current year
the commodity group showing the largest percentage in-
orease in export volume as compared with 1936 has been
the metals, especially iron and steel, tin plate, and
copper. Exports of petroleum produots have been about a
third larger, with much smaller increases for wood pulp,
cotton, and lumber. Shipments of passenger automobiles
have been somewhat smaller, but those of motor trucks
have been 55 percent larger. Exports of leaf tobacco
have fallen abruptly, no shipments being reported since
June.
Cotton. During the first five months of 1937
exports of United States ootton to Japan, averaging
about l40 thousand bales monthly, were much above the
monthly averages for 1935 and 1936 calendar years. At
the end of May, total stocks of raw cotton in Japan
stood at the record level of 960 thousand bales, more
than one and one half times as large as in May 1936.
Succeeding months have witnessed a notable shrinkage
in United Stateo cotton exports to Japan, only 17,049
bales being reported for August, while in October the
value of such shipments was only $1,551,000, about one
fifth of the 1936 monthly average. This decline in the
major

=== Page 372 === (confidence 0.99)
4
major item of United States export trade with Japan is
attributable to several factors: (a) the large reserve
stooks of cotton acoumulated in the early part of l937,
whioh still amounted to 8oo thousand bales at the end
of August 1937; (b) increased imports of Indian and
other foreign cotton, which, for the firet eight months
of 1937, were 356 thousand bales greater than in the
corresponding period of 1936; (c) a slight decline in
Japanese exports of cotton yarn and piece goods, whioh
(a) restrictions on the issuance of permits for imports
of cotton in accordance with the law of September 7 for
temporary management of exports and imports.
Iron, steel and copper. United States exports of
iron, steel and tin scrap to Japan during the first nine
months of the current year have been practically double
the volume for the corresponding period of 1936. The
highest level was reached in June preceding the outbreak
of the North China incident when exports of 257 thousand
tons
1
For the first eight months of l937 Japanese exports of
cotton oloth amounted to 1,666,000,000 square yards
compared to 1,762,000,000 for the same months in 1936.

=== Page 373 === (confidence 0.98)
-5
tons were nearly three times as large as the monthly
average in 1936. Shipments from the United States con-
tinued during July and August above the 200-thousand-ton
mark but fell to about 66 thousand tons in September.
Exports of steel sheets to Japan, which have hitherto
been insignificant, show a nineteen-fold increase during
the present year. Those of wire rods have been doubled
and those of tin plate more than doubled. This movement
has occurred despite the pushing of domestic output of
these steel products to record levels. The Japanese Gov-
ernment is withholding statistics regarding foreign trade
in iron and steel during the present emergency, but the
combined total of imports of all ores and metals during
August reached the unprecedented figure of l06 million
yen. Exports of refined copper and copper scrap to Japan
show better than a 50-percent increase, although shipmento
of lead have declined. Stocks of copper in Japan, which
had fallen from about 3 mill1on metrio tons in June 1936
to 1 1/2 million tons in December, are reported as
2,200,000 tons at the end of July, since which figures
are not being released.
Petroleum products. During the first nine months
of 1937 United States exports of petroleum produots to
Japan aggregated 20.4 million barrels compared to 15
m1ll1on

=== Page 374 === (confidence 0.97)
6
million for the same period of l936. Shipments from the
United States have ranged between 2 and 2 1/2 million
barrels for every month up to the end of September, being
almost entirely in the form of orude oil and gas and fuel
oil. Exports of mineral olls to Kwantung inoreased over
five fold from 233,000 barrels in the first nine months
of 1936 to 1,285,000 in the same period of 1937.
Statistios of stocks of liquid fuels in Japan are not
published, but supplies are apparently ample, with
enlarged imports supplemented by domestic production
of crude petroleum, which rose to l.9 million hecto-
litres during the first half of 1937.
III. Exports to China and Hong Kong.
Petroleum products. Exports of petroleum products
to China during the first three quarters of 1937, while
oquivalent in volume to only about 9 peroent of those
whioh went to Japan in the same period, were double
those of the corresponding months of 1936. From a
monthly average of 115,000 barrels in 1936 they rose
sharply for the present year to 382,500 barrels in Aug-
ust. In September, however, they dropped to 96,200 barrels,
doubtless due to risks attending shipments as the Japan-
ese blockade has been tightened. About two thirds of
this

=== Page 375 === (confidence 0.99)
7
this amount was shipped to Hong Kong in the same month.
The most important items in this group were kerosene and
lubricating oil, which accounted for 70 peroent of the
value of total exports of mineral oils to China and Hong
Kong in January-September 1937. On the other hand, crude
petroleum, which constitutes the bulk of United States
mineral oil exports to Japan, does not appear at all in
suoh exports to China and Hong Kong, whioh are almost
entirely lacking in oil refining facilitles. While United
States shipments of gas and fuel oil to China and Hong
Kong more than doubled in 1937, exports of gasoline, which
alone may be regarded as of military significance, have
been actually smaller.
Metals. Exports of refined copper to China have
doubled in quantity in the nine-month pertod. The speoial
summaries of United States trade with China and Japan pre-
pared by the Department of Commerce do not include figures
of iron and steel exports to China or Hong Kong. Table III,
compiled from work sheets of the Department of Commerce,
shows that the value of United States exports of metals
and manufaotures (excluding machinery and vehicles) to
China and Hong Kong during the first nine months of 1937
amounted to 13.7 million dollars, almost exactly double
that for the same period in 1936.

noted in contrast to the sharp curtailment in such ship-
ments to Japan, it becomes further evident that imports
into China have been much less subjected to Government
control in the interests of military exigencies than in
the case of Japan.

Ootober 6, 1937.
CA - Mr. Green:
FE:
PA/H:

The Seoretary:
Mr. Hartson of the Glenn L. Martin Company telephoned
from Baltimore this morning to inform me that his oompany
had recently turned down an offer from the Japanese Govern-
ment to purohase sixty military airplanes for early delivery.
Mr. Hartson said that he had informed the Japanese Govern-
ment that, due to existing contraets, his company would be
unable to fill this order, but that the aotual reason for
the refusal had been his company's unwillingness to make
any sales to Japan at the present time.
Charles w. Yost

75TH CONGRESS
2D SESSION
MR. HORNBECK
JAN 2 4 1938
IN THE HOUSE OF REPRESENTATIVES
NOVEMBER 23, 1937
Mr. Lewis of Maryland introluced the following joint resolution; which was
referred to the Conimittee on Foreign Affairs and ordered to be printed
894.24/165
JOINT RESOLUTION
Authorizing the President of the U'nited States, in cooperation
with other nations, to apply economic sanctions to Japan.
Whereas the people of the U'nited States are unintentionally yet
effectively assisting Japan in its ruthless invasion and hos-
2
tilities against China, by supplying her with munitions of
war and other materials directly and indirectly aiding her
in her war of aggression against China; and
Whereas such invasion of China by Japan is in flagrant violation
of her specific covenant with the United States and other
0
G
nations in the Nine Power Treaty, to "respect the territorial
integrity and administrative independence" of China, as well
as in violation of her covenant with the United States and
other nations of the world in the Kellogg-Briand Pact to
"renounce war as an instrument of national policy"; and

=== Page 390 === (confidence 0.99)
2
Whereas Japanese aggression in the Far East constitutes a serious
menace to American lives and property in China and a vital
challenge to the “open-door" policy of the United States; and
Whereas, because of the prior and superior treaty obligations
assumed by the United States and the other powers signatory
to the Nine Power Treaty and the Kellogg-Briand Pact, the
Neutrality Act of 1937, being incompatible therewith, is
inapplicable to the present conflict in the Far East ; and
Whereas the President of the U'nited States on October 5 stated
that the world  must be aroused to the cardinal necessity of
honoring sanctity of treaties, of respecting the rights and
liberties of others, and of putting an end to acts of inter-
national aggression": Therefore be it
1
Resolred by the Senate and House of Represcntatires
2 of the United States of America in Congress assembled.
3 That in order to restrain further Japanese aggression in
4 China, to safeguard American interests in that unoffending
5 land, and to restore international law and order, the Presi-
6 dent of the I'nited States is hereby authorized, in coopera-
7 tion with other nations, to employ such economic measures—
8 not including war—but including prohibition by embargoes
9 of the exports from the Inited States to Japan or for her
10 ultimate use, of any munitions of war or such other materials
11 to Japan (except customary foodstuffs used by the Japanese
12 people) as may be used directly or indirectly by her in the
13 prosecution of her aggressions against China and to employ
14 economic sanctions generally against Japan as to the exten-

=== Page 391 === (confidence 1.00)
3
1 sion of credits and export of material as may be calculated to
2 withhold further aid to Japan as an aggressor nation in
3 violating her said covenants with the United States and
4 other nations, and to the end that an early termination of
5 international disorder and violence may be attained by a
6 just and equitable peace.

wounded and 250 killed, all of
whom were non-combatants."
